## motion
verdict: The foundation is unusually disciplined for a third-party notch app and better than most of the paid field: the container genuinely morphs (NotchShape carries `animatableData`, the frame and both corner radii interpolate under one spring — no crossfade cheat on the shell), the window is never resized so nothing rasterizes or flickers across the open, the equalizer runs on CABasicAnimation in the render server with a paused layer clock rather than a SwiftUI timeline, Reduce Motion is honored at 20+ sites with real state changes rather than dead cuts, and there is a unit test (`MotionValuesTests`) standing guard on the damping values. That is a real motion system, not decoration. What keeps it from reading as Apple-built is that the morph stops at the container: there is no `matchedGeometryEffect` or `@Namespace` anywhere in the 66 files, so the album art, the equalizer and the title do not travel from the pill into the panel — they crossfade at two unrelated positions and sizes, which is exactly the thing Apple's Dynamic Island is famous for not doing. Second, the interaction that drives the motion has a dead input: the click that opens the island cannot close it, and a click-opened panel deliberately ignores the pointer leaving while Escape cannot reach it on the Music tab. Third, the app has zero SF Symbol replace effects and zero numeric text transitions, so every play/pause and every second of the clock hard-cuts — the two most-watched pixels in the app are the two least Apple-native. Fix those three and the motion clears the native bar; the rest of the list is polish.
- [CONFIRMED] (major/L) The container morphs but the contents crossfade — no matchedGeometryEffect anywhere in the app
    today: Clicking the island grows a black shape from roughly 284×32 to 560×208 with continuously interpolated corner radii, which is correct. But the 22 pt cover art sitting at the notch shrinks to 0.9 and fades out in place, while a completely different 118 pt cover art fades up ~120 pt to the left and ~60 pt down. Same album, two objects. Same for the equalizer bars (compact right wing → open header trailing) and the title (peek left wing → MediaPane text column).
    apple: Apple's Dynamic Island is built on exactly this morph: the compact leading and trailing regions are the *same* elements as the expanded leading/trailing regions, and on expansion the album art translates and scales along a continuous path into its expanded slot while the container's blob geometry stretches around it. The HIG's Live Activities guidance is explicit that the compact and expanded presentations should read as one object changing size, not two presentations swapping. macOS 26's own Liquid Glass morphs (GlassEffectContainer + `glassEffectID`) apply the same rule to AppKit-era surfaces.
    impact: Every open reads as "a panel appeared" instead of "the island unfolded". This is the single most-noticed difference against NotchNook and Alcove, both of which move the artwork, and against the iPhone island every buyer is comparing it to. A reviewer's first sentence about this app will be about this transition.
    evidence: `grep -rn "matchedGeometryEffect|@Namespace|Namespace.ID" Sources/` returns zero hits across all 66 files. Compact artwork is a 22×22 box with corner radius 6, centred in the left wing: Sources/IslaKit/UI/NotchContentView.swift:370 `.frame(width: 22, height: 22)` / :371 `RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: 6, style: .continuous)`. Open artwork is a separate, unrelated view — 118×118, radius 14, laid out after the 14 pt padding and the 30 pt rail: Sources/IslaKit/UI/MediaPane.swift:203 `.frame(width: 118, height: 118)` / :204 `RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius: 14, style: .continuous)`. The handoff between them is a pure crossfade: Sources/IslaKit/UI/NotchContentView.swift:151 `compactMediaHeader.transition(Theme.scaleIn(0.9, reduceMotion: reduceMotion))` on the way out, and Sources/IslaKit/UI/NotchContentView.swift:96-98 `if isOpen { content.transition(.opacity) }` on the way in. The shell itself does morph correctly — Sources/IslaKit/UI/NotchShape.swift:10-16 exposes `animatableData: AnimatablePair<CGFloat, CGFloat>` for both radii, and NotchContentView.swift:76 animates `.frame(width: size.width + 2 * topRadius, height: size.height)` — so the gap is content-only.
    fix: Add `@Namespace private var island` to `NotchContentView` and give the three elements that exist in both states a shared identity: `compactArtwork` (NotchContentView.swift:335) and `MediaPane.artwork(for:)` (MediaPane.swift:165) both get `.matchedGeometryEffect(id: "artwork", in: island)`; the compact `EqualizerBars` (NotchContentView.swift:380) and the open header's `EqualizerBars` (NotchContentView.swift:398) share `id: "equalizer"`. This requires hoisting the namespace through `MediaPane` as an `@Namespace.ID` parameter, and lifting the artwork out of the `if isOpen` / `else if compactActivity.isVisible` branch structure so both source and destination are in the same view tree for at least one frame — the cleanest shape is a single `artworkView` whose frame/radius are driven by `isOpen` rather than two independent views. Corner radius and size then interpolate for free under the existing `Theme.openAnimation`. While in here, reconsider `dampingFraction: 1.0` for the *open* specifically (Theme.swift:17): Apple's island morph carries a small settle (bounce ≈ 0.15, i.e. `.spring(duration: 0.34, bounce: 0.15)`), which is what makes it read as one physical object stretching; keep 1.0 for `compactAnimation`, where nothing is being grabbed. `MotionValuesTests.testNothingWithoutMomentumOvershoots` would need amending with a stated reason if you take that.
- [CONFIRMED] (major/M) The click that opens the island cannot close it, and a click-opened panel ignores the pointer leaving
    today: Click the island on the Music tab. It opens and pins. Move the mouse away: nothing. Click the island again: nothing at all — the gesture is swallowed with no visual acknowledgement. Press Escape: nothing, the panel is not key. The only exits are a left/right mouse-down inside some *other* application (the global monitor at NotchController.swift:566-573) or the ⌥⌘I hotkey. A 620×208 black panel sits over the top of the screen while you keep typing.
    apple: On the Dynamic Island, tapping the expanded presentation acts (opens the app) and swiping it up dismisses it — the element you touched to open is never inert. Every macOS transient surface Apple ships — NSPopover `.transient`, Control Centre, Notification Centre, the menu bar extras — closes on Escape whether or not it holds first responder, and closes on a second click of the control that opened it. macOS also collapses a hovered-open element when the pointer leaves.
    impact: The most obvious way to dismiss the thing you just opened does nothing, with no feedback that the input was even received. Users will click the island repeatedly, conclude it is stuck, and reach for the menu bar — which isn't there. Against Alcove and NotchNook, both of which collapse on mouse-out, this reads as a hang.
    evidence: The shell's gesture refuses every click once open: Sources/IslaKit/UI/NotchContentView.swift:119 `guard !isOpen else { return }`. The controller refuses too: Sources/IslaKit/Notch/NotchController.swift:500 `guard !vm.isOpen else { return }`. Opening by click pins the panel: NotchController.swift:517 `vm.isPinnedOpen = true`, and `holdsOpen` is just that flag (Sources/IslaKit/Model/NotchViewModel.swift:69). The pointer handler then refuses to close a pinned panel — NotchController.swift:753 `if !inside, self.viewModel?.holdsOpen == true { return }` — and, with the default Open-on-Hover off, it returns before it can ever clear the pin: NotchController.swift:731 `if inside, !NotchViewModel.opensOnHoverEnabled { return }` sits above NotchController.swift:734-736 `self.viewModel?.isPinnedOpen = false`. Escape cannot reach it on the Music tab either: `panel.onEscape` (NotchController.swift:658) is dispatched from `NotchPanel.sendEvent` (Sources/IslaKit/Notch/NotchPanel.swift:82), which only sees keyDown while the window is key, and `canBecomeKey` is `acceptsKeyboard` (NotchPanel.swift:28) — true only on Translate.
    fix: Three changes. (1) In `NotchContentView`'s `.onEnded` (NotchContentView.swift:119), drop the `guard !isOpen`, restrict the hit test to the collapsed notch strip when open (the `header` band, `vm.geometry.notchSize.height` tall) and call `vm.onIslandClick?()` regardless; in `islandClicked` (NotchController.swift:494) replace `guard !vm.isOpen else { return }` with `if vm.isOpen { vm.isPinnedOpen = false; setOpen(false); pointer.setInside(false); updatePinnedClickMonitor(); return }` so the same click toggles. Because `setOpen(false)` defers `collapse()` one runloop pass and `openGeneration` already stamps it, a re-click mid-collapse retargets rather than queues. (2) Move the pin clear above the hover-enabled early return in `pointer.onChange` (NotchController.swift:731) so the pointer arriving on the panel hands control back even when Open-on-Hover is off — the comment at NotchController.swift:733-734 already says that is the intent. (3) Install an `NSEvent.addGlobalMonitorForEvents(matching: .keyDown)` alongside `pinnedClickMonitor` (NotchController.swift:558) that closes on keyCode 53, so Escape works on every tab and not only where the panel is key. Extend `OpenOnClickTests` with a toggle case.
- [CONFIRMED] (major/S) Play/pause and every other symbol state change hard-cuts — no symbolEffect or contentTransition in the codebase
    today: Press play. The pause glyph is replaced by the play glyph on a single frame — a hard cut, with the surrounding button's press dimming (ModeToggle.swift:51-53) as the only motion. Cycle repeat and the `repeat` glyph becomes `repeat.1` instantly.
    apple: Since macOS 14, `.contentTransition(.symbolEffect(.replace))` is the system behavior for exactly this, and Apple applies it everywhere a transport glyph changes: Music, Podcasts, TV, the Control Centre Now Playing module, and the menu bar Now Playing widget. The play↔pause pair specifically uses the down-up replace variant so the old glyph sinks as the new one rises. It is the single most recognizable micro-animation in Apple's media UI.
    impact: The one control users touch dozens of times a day has no motion at all, next to a lock card and a panel that are otherwise carefully animated. It is the fastest tell that this is not an Apple app, and it costs one modifier to fix.
    evidence: `grep -rn "symbolEffect|contentTransition|SymbolEffect" Sources/IslaKit` returns zero hits. The transport swaps glyphs bare: Sources/IslaKit/UI/MediaPane.swift:341-343 `Image(systemName: media.isPlaying ? "pause.fill" : "play.fill")`. The lock card does the same (Sources/IslaKit/UI/LockScreenCard.swift transport row). `ModeToggle` swaps repeat/repeat.1 with only a colour animation: Sources/IslaKit/UI/ModeToggle.swift:21-22 `Image(systemName: symbol)` under `.animation(Theme.contentAnimation, value: isOn)` at :32 — the animation covers `isOn`, not the glyph identity, so `repeat` → `repeat.1` is a raw substitution. The compact artwork's play badge does get a transition (NotchContentView.swift:367 `Theme.scaleIn(0.82,...)`) but the glyph inside it does not.
    fix: Add `.contentTransition(.symbolEffect(.replace.downUp))` to the play/pause `Image` at MediaPane.swift:342 and to its twin on the lock card, and `.contentTransition(.symbolEffect(.replace))` to `ModeToggle`'s `Image(systemName: symbol)` at ModeToggle.swift:21 and to the compact play badge glyph at NotchContentView.swift:359. Gate on Reduce Motion the same way the rest of the file does — `reduceMotion ? .identity : .symbolEffect(.replace.downUp)` — matching the pattern at Theme.swift:44-46. macOS 15 is the deployment target so no availability check is needed.
    challenges: Facts are right: `grep -rn "symbolEffect|contentTransition|SymbolEffect" Sources/` returns zero hits; MediaPane.swift:341-342 swaps `pause.fill`/`play.fill` bare inside `TransportGlyphStyle`, whose only motion is press dimming plus a 0.92 scale (ModeToggle.swift:49-52); MediaPane.swift:359 swaps `repeat.1`/`repeat` under `ModeToggle`'s `.animation(Theme.contentAnimation, value: isOn)` (ModeToggle.swift:32), which cannot animate glyph identity. But severity is inflated. The button already answers the press with dimming and scale, the state change is unambiguous, and no user has ever named a missing SF Symbol replace effect as a reason not to use an app. This is textbook cosmetic parity with Apple — a one-line `.contentTransition(.symbolEffect(.replace))` that improves a demo GIF, not a complaint anyone files. | corrected: polish — play/pause and repeat glyphs hard-cut instead of using `.contentTransition(.symbolEffect(.replace))`; press dimming and scale already carry the feedback, so nothing is unclear, only unpolished.
- [CONFIRMED] (minor/S) Panel content enters and leaves on the same 0.34 s spring as the container — the asymmetry the code already knows about is used only for tab swaps
    today: On open, the rail icons, artwork, scrubber and transport ramp up in opacity on a critically damped spring starting at t=0, so they are visible at partial alpha while the container is still under half its final height, clipped mid-glyph by the `.clipped()` at NotchContentView.swift:102. On close, the same content lingers at 40-60 % alpha for several hundred milliseconds as the panel folds back through it.
    apple: Designing Fluid Interfaces and every Apple container transition stage the two: the container's geometry leads, the incoming content follows a beat behind it (a 30-60 ms delay), and on dismissal the content leaves first and faster than the container, so the container is empty before it shrinks. Apple's Dynamic Island expanded content appears once the blob has essentially arrived; the compact content leaves in about a third of the collapse duration.
    impact: The open reads slightly mushy rather than crisp — content and container arrive as one soft ramp instead of a container that snaps into place and then fills. On close, text is visibly sliding up through the notch. It's the difference between "animated" and "choreographed".
    evidence: The body is inserted and removed with a plain symmetric opacity transition: Sources/IslaKit/UI/NotchContentView.swift:96-99 `if isOpen { content.transition(.opacity) }`, resolved by the innermost matching animation modifier, Sources/IslaKit/UI/NotchContentView.swift:130 `.animation(Theme.open(reduceMotion: reduceMotion), value: isOpen)` — i.e. `Animation.spring(response: 0.34, dampingFraction: 1.0)` (Theme.swift:17). The same file already ships the correct asymmetric pattern one screen down, but only for pane switching: NotchContentView.swift:446-453 `.transition(.asymmetric(insertion: .opacity.combined(with: .scale(scale: 0.97)).animation(Theme.paneIn), removal: .opacity.combined(with: .scale(scale: 1.02)).animation(Theme.paneOut)))`, with `paneIn = .easeOut(duration: 0.20).delay(0.04)` and `paneOut = .easeIn(duration: 0.12)` (Theme.swift:25-26). Theme.swift:22-23 even states the rule — "the outgoing pane leaves faster than the incoming one arrives" — and then does not apply it to the open/close.
    fix: Replace NotchContentView.swift:98's `.transition(.opacity)` with the asymmetric pair already in Theme: `.transition(.asymmetric(insertion: .opacity.animation(Theme.paneIn), removal: .opacity.animation(Theme.paneOut)))`. Do the same for the `openHeader`/`compactMediaHeader` swap at NotchContentView.swift:146-156, where the compact header should leave on `paneOut` rather than on the open spring. If the artwork morph from `no-pill-to-panel-morph` lands, exclude the matched element from this — a morphing view must not also be fading.
- [CONFIRMED] (minor/S) The lyrics page advances a 48 pt line in a 0.16 s ease-out, where Apple Music uses a spring with a settle
    today: Each new lyric line yanks the column up 48 pt in 160 ms with no settle, then stops dead. The karaoke sweep inside the line is still interpolating over 250 ms when the scroll has already finished.
    apple: Apple Music's lyrics view advances on a spring with a visible settle — closer to a 0.45 s response with a small bounce — and the lines stagger slightly rather than moving as one rigid block, which is what makes the page feel like it is being carried by the song rather than being repositioned by a timer. Spotify's desktop lyrics do the same.
    impact: On the app's marquee feature — the one nothing else on the Mac has — the page reads as jumping rather than flowing, which undercuts the whole "riding the same clock" premise the file's own doc comment makes. It is the difference between a karaoke surface you want to leave open and one you glance at.
    evidence: The advance is Sources/IslaKit/UI/LyricsStage.swift:297-302 `center(on:)` → `withAnimation(Theme.contentAnimation) { reading = id }`, and `Theme.contentAnimation = Animation.easeOut(duration: 0.16)` (Sources/IslaKit/UI/Theme.swift:21). One line of travel is `slotHeight: CGFloat = 40` plus `slotSpacing: CGFloat = 8` (LyricsStage.swift:196-197) = 48 pt, so peak velocity is roughly 300 pt/s. The same 0.16 s ease is also what fades the row's current/non-current styling (LyricsStage.swift:378) and the sweep interpolation is separately `.linear(duration: 0.25)` (Sources/IslaKit/UI/KaraokeText.swift:108), so the page motion is faster than the word motion it is supposed to be carrying.
    fix: Give the stage its own curve rather than borrowing the generic 0.16 s ease. Add `static let lyricScroll = Animation.spring(response: 0.42, dampingFraction: 0.86)` to `Theme` (with a Reduce Motion variant alongside `Theme.open`/`Theme.compact`) and use it at LyricsStage.swift:301. Leave LyricsStage.swift:378's row styling on `contentAnimation` — the fade should still be quick — so only the travel gains the settle. A slight bounce is defensible here under the file's own rule: the page is being carried by the song's momentum, not opened by a click.
- [CONFIRMED] (polish/S) Every clock and counter digit hard-cuts once per second
    today: `1:23` becomes `1:24` as a frame-boundary substitution, four times a second's worth of position updates collapsing into one visible cut per second. The counter beside SHELF jumps from 3 to 4 the same way.
    apple: `.contentTransition(.numericText())` shipped in macOS 13 specifically for this and Apple applies it to the Music scrubber times, the Podcasts remaining-time label, the Clock app, and the Now Playing widget: digits roll vertically in the direction the number is moving. `.numericText(countsDown: true)` handles the remaining-time label.
    impact: A small thing, but it is on screen the entire time a track plays and sits right under a scrubber that *is* smooth (advancing sub-pixel at the 4 Hz tick, MediaController.swift:1107-1113). The mismatch between a gliding bar and a stuttering clock is the kind of detail that reads as unfinished next to a $25 app.
    evidence: The elapsed/duration labels are monospaced but untransitioned: Sources/IslaKit/UI/MediaPane.swift:306 `.font(.system(size: 10, weight: .medium).monospacedDigit())` with `Text(formatTime(...))` above it at :303. Same on the lock card (Sources/IslaKit/UI/LockScreenCard.swift:501) and the lyrics stage offset readout (Sources/IslaKit/UI/LyricsStage.swift:89, :409). The shelf/clipboard counters in the header do the same: Sources/IslaKit/UI/NotchContentView.swift:419-422 `Text("\(value)").font(.system(size: 10, weight: .medium).monospacedDigit())`. `grep -rn "contentTransition|numericText" Sources/IslaKit` returns nothing.
    fix: Add `.contentTransition(.numericText())` to the elapsed `Text` in MediaPane's time row (MediaPane.swift, the `formatTime` labels feeding the `.font(...)` at :306), to LockScreenCard.swift:501, and `.contentTransition(.numericText(value: Double(value)))` to `counter(_:)` at NotchContentView.swift:420. The transition needs an animation in scope to play — wrap the position publisher's effect with `.animation(.default, value: media.position)` on the label only, not on the bar, since MediaPane.swift:234-236 deliberately keeps the fill unanimated so a seek lands under the cursor. Gate on Reduce Motion like the rest of the file.
    challenges: Every code citation is exact — MediaPane.swift:303/306 (`Text(formatTime(...))` under `.font(.system(size: 10, weight: .medium).monospacedDigit())`), LockScreenCard.swift:501, LyricsStage.swift:89 and :409, NotchContentView.swift:419-422 — and `grep -rn "contentTransition|numericText" Sources/` returns nothing. The 4 Hz detail is even corroborated by KaraokeText.swift:106-108 ("One tick of the position ticker", 0.25 s). What does not survive skepticism is the implied Apple half. `.contentTransition(.numericText())` is real on macOS 13+, but Apple's own transport time labels do not use it: Music, Podcasts and the Control Center Now Playing module all substitute elapsed-time digits without a roll. Apple reserves numericText for large, glanceable readouts (Clock, Stopwatch, Fitness, Lock Screen) and for counts/badges. Applying it to a 10 pt monospaced elapsed-time label ticking once a second would be more motion than Apple ships, not less. | corrected: Only the count readouts are a real native gap — the SHELF/CLIPBOARD counters at NotchContentView.swift:419-422, where Apple animates badge counts. The elapsed/duration labels at MediaPane.swift:303-306, LockScreenCard.swift:501 and the lyrics offset readouts at LyricsStage.swift:89/:409 hard-cut in Apple's own players too; leaving them un-rolled is matching Apple, not falling short of it.
- [DISPUTED] (minor/S) The hover lift is stretched across the whole expanding panel with the collapsed corner radii while it fades
    today: For the ~0.35 s of the open, two white gradient washes (`liftFill`, 1.5 %→7.5 % white, NotchContentView.swift:301-305) scale up from pill-sized to panel-sized and fade out over the expanding panel, clipped to a near-square outline that does not match the panel's animating 12/22 radii. Because the click path sets `vm.isHovering = false` and `isOpen = true` in the same pass (NotchController.swift:512-521), this happens on every single click-open, not just on hover.
    apple: A highlight is a property of the surface it lights, so Apple's pressed/hovered states resolve on the element's own geometry and are dropped fast — a hover highlight never survives into the transition that replaces the element. Where a highlight does persist across a morph, it is clipped by the same animating shape as the surface underneath it.
    impact: A faint pale bloom sweeps outward across the panel on every open, with corners that visibly disagree with the panel's own. It is subtle — 7.5 % white at most, with a falloff mask — but it is the reason the open has a slightly "washed" first third rather than a clean fill.
    evidence: `hoverLift` is removed by `if !isOpen { hoverLift }` (Sources/IslaKit/UI/NotchContentView.swift:92) and leaves via `.transition(.opacity)` (NotchContentView.swift:286) resolved against the open spring at NotchContentView.swift:130. But while it is departing it is still laid out from the *animating* body size — NotchContentView.swift:254 `let wingWidth = max(0, (size.width - vm.geometry.notchSize.width) / 2 + topRadius)` and :273 `.frame(width: size.width + 2 * topRadius, height: size.height)`, where `size` is `vm.bodySize` (NotchContentView.swift:8) growing from ~284×32 to 560×208 — while its clip is hardcoded to the collapsed silhouette: NotchContentView.swift:274-279 `.clipShape(NotchShape(topRadius: Theme.collapsedTopRadius, bottomRadius: Theme.collapsedBottomRadius))`, i.e. radii 6 and 9 applied to a 560×208 box.
    fix: Two options, both cheap. Cleanest: make the clip follow the container by passing the same computed `topRadius`/bottom radius into the `clipShape` at NotchContentView.swift:274-279 instead of the hardcoded `Theme.collapsedTopRadius`/`collapsedBottomRadius`, matching what the shape at :71-73 already does. Better still, drop the lift instantly rather than over the open spring: give it `.transition(.asymmetric(insertion: .opacity, removal: .opacity.animation(.easeOut(duration: 0.10))))` at NotchContentView.swift:286, so the highlight is gone before the panel has travelled far enough for the geometry mismatch to be visible. The same hardcoded-radius issue exists on `compactWingSurface` (NotchContentView.swift:325-330) but is invisible there because the wings are `Color.black` on a black shape.
    challenges: Every cited line is real — NotchContentView.swift:92 `if !isOpen { hoverLift }`, :254 wingWidth from `size`, :273 `.frame(width: size.width + 2 * topRadius, ...)`, :274-279 clip hardcoded to `Theme.collapsedTopRadius`/`collapsedBottomRadius`, :286 `.transition(.opacity)`, and NotchController.swift:514/:517/:522 setting `isHovering = false` and `isOpen = true` in one pass. But the claimed consequence does not follow: `size` is `vm.bodySize`, and NotchViewModel.swift:219-221 returns `openBodySize` *only when `isOpen` is true* — the exact condition under which line 92 removes the lift. A view removed under a transition renders its last-evaluated geometry, so the departing wash stays at the collapsed pill size (~296×32) and the radii 6/9 at :276-277 match the box they clip. There is no 560×208 box wearing collapsed corners. | corrected: On every click-open the hover lift fades out over the ~0.34 s open spring (NotchContentView.swift:286 resolved against :130-131) rather than leaving promptly, so a white wash lingers at pill size over the front of the expanding panel. Its clip radii are hardcoded (:274-279) rather than read from the animating `topRadius`, which is currently harmless because the lift only ever exists while `!isOpen` — but it is a latent coupling if the lift is ever shown over an open panel. ;; The code facts are exact — NotchContentView.swift:92 `if !isOpen { hoverLift }`, :286 `.transition(.opacity)` resolved against the open spring at :130, :254 and :273 laid out from `size`/`topRadius`, :274-279 clipping to hardcoded `Theme.collapsedTopRadius`/`collapsedBottomRadius` (6 and 9 per Theme.swift:76-77), :301-305 `liftFill` at 1.5%→7.5% white — and the click path really does set both flags in one pass (NotchController.swift:514 `vm.isHovering = false`, :522 `setOpen(true)`), so it fires on every click-open. But the described artifact depends on SwiftUI re-laying-out a view that is being removed, which it does not do: when `isOpen` flips, `hoverLift` is absent from the new tree, so SwiftUI retains the previous view value with its frame already baked at the collapsed `size` and `topRadius` (`bodySize` is computed from `isOpen` at NotchViewModel.swift:219-227, so there is no intermediate pass where the lift is alive at the open size). It fades out at pill dimensions, where the hardcoded collapsed radii are the *correct* silhouette — hoverLift can only ever exist while `!isOpen`, i.e. while `topRadius == collapsedTopRadius`. The lingering fade is also deliberate and documented at NotchController.swift:512-513 ("The hover lift goes out with the same animation that opens the panel, rather than snapping off the instant `isOpen` flips"). | corrected: There is no stretched wash and no radius mismatch. What actually happens is that a pill-sized bright patch sits at the top of the panel fading out on the 0.34 s open spring while the panel has already grown past it — a deliberate cross-fade (NotchController.swift:512-513) whose only real cost is that a 0.34 s critically-damped spring is a long tail for a hover highlight, and that the press boost (`.opacity(isPressed ? 2.2 : 1)`, NotchContentView.swift:283) is what it fades down from. ;; The code citations are accurate — `if !isOpen { hoverLift }` (NotchContentView.swift:92), `.transition(.opacity)` (NotchContentView.swift:286) against the open spring (NotchContentView.swift:130), size from `vm.bodySize` (NotchContentView.swift:8) via NotchContentView.swift:254 and :273, clip hardcoded to `Theme.collapsedTopRadius`/`collapsedBottomRadius` (NotchContentView.swift:274-279) — but the stated mechanism is unproven. SwiftUI freezes a removed subtree's content at its last evaluated values; the departing `hoverLift` holds the collapsed `wingWidth`/frame it was built with, so what most likely renders is a pill-sized wash fading out over a growing panel, not a wash stretching to 560x208 under near-square radii. Either way the artifact is `liftFill` at 1.5%-7.5% white over black (NotchContentView.swift:301-305), fading to zero across ~340 ms while the panel expands and content fades in over it — below the threshold at which anybody names it, and the claim's own most-visible symptom (the mismatched radii on a big box) is the part that probably does not happen. | corrected: polish — the hover lift fades out across the open instead of ending with the collapsed state; the wash is 1.5-7.5% white over 340 ms and the claimed stretch-to-panel-size is not established, since SwiftUI freezes removed subtrees at their last layout.
- [CONFIRMED] (polish/S) The lock card window pops in and out with no transition at all
    today: Lock the Mac. The shield goes up, then a moment later a 460×300 glass card materialises in the dead centre of the display in a single frame. Unlock and it vanishes the same way.
    apple: macOS's own lock-screen Now Playing card fades in with the shield rather than appearing after it. Every system surface that arrives over the shield — the card, the password field, the hint — arrives on a crossfade. Apple does not hard-cut a 460 pt object onto a screen the user is looking at.
    impact: The one moment the app is the only thing on screen is also its least polished transition. It reads as a window being created rather than as part of the lock screen, which is the impression this feature is entirely built to avoid.
    evidence: Sources/IslaKit/Notch/LockCardWindow.swift:99 `panel.orderFrontRegardless()` puts a 484×324 window on screen at full opacity on the frame the `com.apple.screenIsLocked` notification lands; Sources/IslaKit/Notch/LockCardWindow.swift:106-110 `dismiss` calls `panel.orderOut(nil)` and tears the window down. The panel suppresses AppKit's own window animation: Sources/IslaKit/Notch/NotchPanel.swift:156 `animationBehavior = .none`. Nothing sets `alphaValue` or uses `NSAnimationContext` anywhere — `grep -rn "NSAnimationContext|alphaValue|animator\(\)" Sources/IslaKit` returns only `orderFrontRegardless` sites. The SwiftUI side is deliberately frozen too (NotchContentView.swift:60-61 `.animation(nil, value: vm.isLockedPresentation)` / `.transaction { $0.animation = nil }`), but that comment is about the *notch panel's* stretched-snapshot bug, not about this separate window.
    fix: In `LockCardWindow.present` (LockCardWindow.swift:99), set `panel.alphaValue = 0` before `orderFrontRegardless()` then `NSAnimationContext.runAnimationGroup { $0.duration = 0.22; $0.timingFunction = CAMediaTimingFunction(name: .easeOut); panel.animator().alphaValue = 1 }`; in `dismiss` (LockCardWindow.swift:106) run the reverse at 0.15 s with the `orderOut` and teardown in the completion handler. This is safe with respect to the bug the type's doc comment guards against — that was a *resize* across the shield transition producing a stretched window-server snapshot, and alpha does not change the frame. Honor Reduce Transparency/Reduce Motion by checking `NSWorkspace.shared.accessibilityDisplayShouldReduceMotion` and skipping straight to `alphaValue = 1`, matching `SystemAppearance` (Sources/IslaKit/Services/SystemAppearance.swift:30).

## materials
verdict: The material *thinking* here is better than almost any shipping third-party Mac app: `GlassSurface` is a single named recipe with a real elevation vocabulary, it correctly refuses to stack a drawn scrim under Apple's own `glassEffect` (GlassSurface.swift:186-193), it lays `NSVisualEffectView(.hudWindow)` underneath rather than hand-faking a blur, Reduce Transparency is routed to a genuine opaque fallback, and `Theme.tertiary` carries a hand-computed 4.58:1 justification (Theme.swift:83-87). That is real craft. But the system reaches exactly three call sites — the lyrics sync pill and two surfaces on the lock card (`rg 'glassSurface('` → LyricsStage.swift:336, LockScreenCard.swift:105, 381). The panel the user actually looks at all day is `NotchShape().fill(Color.black)` (NotchContentView.swift:75) with one hand-tuned shadow, no material, no option, and no awareness of any accessibility display setting. Increase Contrast is published by `SystemAppearance` and consumed by exactly two `strokeBorder` calls in one file. Non-text UI — the selected tab, the selected segment, the scrubber track, every hairline — sits between 1.1:1 and 1.4:1 against its own background, well under the 3:1 the platform holds itself to. And the one place the app is most proud of, the lock card, is the surface that degrades on macOS 26, because `preferDrawn` was written for exactly that case and is passed by nobody. Close to Apple-native in intent; roughly two thirds of the way there in reach.
- [DISPUTED] (major/S) On macOS 26 the lock card asks for Liquid Glass above the login shield, where nothing can be sampled — and the escape hatch built for it is passed nowhere
    today: On macOS 15 the lock card gets VibrancyBackdrop + a black base gradient + rim + grain, and white 18pt type reads over any wallpaper. On macOS 26 the same code takes the `glassEffect` branch, which supplies no drawn base at all — if the shield denies the backdrop (which the code's own comment asserts), the 460×300 card renders as a near-clear lens and its white type sits directly on the lock wallpaper. The only remedy shipped is an undocumented `defaults write dev.dynamicisland.app drawnGlass -bool true`, which also kills real glass everywhere else.
    apple: Apple's own lock-screen Now Playing widget is drawn opaque-backed for exactly this reason — it never relies on sampling protected content. Liquid Glass on macOS 26 is never shipped on a control whose legibility depends on a backdrop that may not exist.
    impact: A user on macOS 26 with a bright wallpaper locks the Mac and the player is white text floating on the wallpaper with no pane behind it — the flagship feature of the app looks broken on the newest OS, and there is no setting to fix it (the Card Style → Solid option routes through a different branch and still stacks `.ultraThinMaterial`, LockScreenCard.swift:113-123, which also samples nothing there).
    evidence: GlassSurface.swift:14-18 states the constraint: "there is one place it can never work: above the login shield, which is protected content that no window is given a backdrop for." GlassSurface.swift:200-202 adds the remedy: `// Force the drawn recipe even where the real material exists. For the one surface that cannot sample anything: above the login shield. preferDrawn: Bool = false`. `rg 'preferDrawn' Sources` returns only GlassSurface.swift itself — no call site ever passes it. LockScreenCard.swift:105-112 calls `.glassSurface(cornerRadius: 30, elevation: .card, samplesBackdrop: style == .glass)`, so on macOS 26 `usesSystemGlass` (GlassSurface.swift:232-237) is true and GlassSurface.swift:244 runs `content.glassEffect(glass, in: .rect(cornerRadius: cornerRadius))` — which skips the whole drawn recipe including its opaque base (`.card` base black 0.34→0.16, GlassSurface.swift:39-45). GlassRoutingTests.swift:36-38 admits it: "the escape hatch for the lock card: it draws above the login shield, and whether the real material finds anything to sample there cannot be settled from a build machine."
    fix: Pass `preferDrawn: true` at LockScreenCard.swift:105 unconditionally, or gate it on a one-time runtime probe. The card is the one surface the file's own doc comment names as unable to sample; there is no case where Apple's material helps it. Keep `samplesBackdrop: style == .glass` so the VibrancyBackdrop is still laid under it for the (harmless) case where the shield does vend one. Add a test asserting `LockScreenCard`'s style routes to `drawn` on every OS, so this cannot regress.
    challenges: The structural half is exact: GlassSurface.swift:15-18 states the shield constraint; :200-202 declares `preferDrawn: Bool = false`; `rg preferDrawn Sources` returns only GlassSurface.swift:202/210/223/233 — no call site passes it; LockScreenCard.swift:105-112 passes `samplesBackdrop: style == .glass` with style defaulting to `.glass` (LockScreenCard.swift:53), so on macOS 26 usesSystemGlass (GlassSurface.swift:232-237) is true and :244 runs `content.glassEffect(...)`, skipping the `.card` base black 0.34→0.16 at :39-45. Two parts are wrong. (1) The `defaults write` is NOT the only remedy: SettingsPane.swift:168-180 ships a user-facing `Card Style` Glass/Solid picker, and choosing Solid sets samplesBackdrop=false, which forces the drawn recipe *for the lock card alone* plus an `.ultraThinMaterial` + black-0.45 backing (LockScreenCard.swift:113-123) — a one-click, card-scoped fix. It is also documented, in checklist.md:92 (and the bundle id there is com.ctimothe.isla, not dev.dynamicisland.app). (2) The type is not naked on the wallpaper: LockScreenCard.swift:97-102 applies a per-mark shadow (`.black.opacity(style == .glass ? 0.55 : 0), radius: 4, y: 1`) enabled precisely in glass mode, with a comment saying this is how white type survives a bright wallpaper without a scrim — the same technique the system lock clock uses. | corrected: On macOS 26 the lock card defaults to `glassEffect` above the login shield with no drawn base underneath, and the per-surface `preferDrawn` escape hatch (GlassSurface.swift:202) is passed by zero call sites — only tests. If the shield denies the backdrop, the card's substrate is whatever Apple's material falls back to, defended only by a per-mark drop shadow (LockScreenCard.swift:102). Two remedies exist, not one: the in-app `Card Style → Solid` picker (SettingsPane.swift:170), which is card-scoped, and the global `drawnGlass` default (checklist.md:92), which is not. ;; The mechanism checks out: GlassSurface.swift:200-202 declares `preferDrawn` and `rg preferDrawn Sources` returns only GlassSurface.swift:202/210/223/233 — no call site passes it, only GlassRoutingTests.swift:32 exercises it. LockScreenCard.swift:105-112 passes `samplesBackdrop: style == .glass` with `.glass` the default (LockScreenCard.swift:53, NotchViewModel.swift:286), so on macOS 26 GlassSurface.swift:233-237 returns true and :244 takes `glassEffect`, skipping the drawn base at :39-45. `glassEffect`/`Glass.regular.tint` are real macOS 26 API, and the premise is honestly hedged (GlassSurface.swift:14-18, GlassRoutingTests.swift:35-38 both concede it is unverified from a build machine). But 'the only remedy shipped is an undocumented defaults write' is false, and 'white type sits directly on the lock wallpaper' understates the mitigation. | corrected: Accurate version: on macOS 26 the default lock card takes Apple's `glassEffect` above the login shield, where the app's own comment says nothing can be sampled, and the per-surface escape hatch built for exactly that case (`preferDrawn`) is passed by no call site — so the default path is unverified on hardware. Two mitigations do ship, however. Settings > Card Style > Solid (SettingsPane.swift:167-181, NotchViewModel.LockCardStyle at NotchViewModel.swift:265-282, documented in-code as 'for the wallpapers glass cannot win against') sets `samplesBackdrop: false`, which bypasses `glassEffect` entirely and adds `.ultraThinMaterial` + black 0.45 under the drawn base (LockScreenCard.swift:113-121) — a localized, discoverable, lock-card-scoped fix, not a global kill switch. And every mark on the card already carries a 0.55/r4/y1 shadow while in glass style (LockScreenCard.swift:102), the same technique Apple's own lock-screen clock uses to survive a bright wallpaper. Also, the defaults domain is `com.ctimothe.isla` (NotchViewModel.swift:332), not `dev.dynamicisland.app`. ;; Mechanism confirmed: Sources/IslaKit/UI/LockScreenCard.swift:105-112 passes no `preferDrawn`, and `rg preferDrawn Sources` hits only GlassSurface.swift:202/210/223/233, so on macOS 26 GlassSurface.swift:244 takes the `glassEffect` branch and skips the drawn base. But the claim omits two shipped mitigations. LockScreenCard.swift:102 puts a `.black.opacity(0.55) radius 4 y1` shadow on *every mark* of the card precisely for this case (its comment: 'how white type survives a bright wallpaper without a scrim' — the same thing Apple's own lock screen does), so the worst case is a card that loses its pane, not type that becomes unreadable. And SettingsPane.swift:170 ships a user-facing `Card Style` → Solid, which routes to `samplesBackdrop: false` → the drawn recipe plus `.ultraThinMaterial` + `.black.opacity(0.45)` (LockScreenCard.swift:113-121) — a real remedy that is not the undocumented default. The defaults-write domain quoted is also stale: NotchViewModel.swift:332 says `com.ctimothe.isla`, not `dev.dynamicisland.app`. Finally the premise is unverified by the codebase's own admission (GlassRoutingTests.swift:36-38). | corrected: minor — unverified risk that on macOS 26 the lock card renders as a near-clear lens above the shield; type stays legible via the per-mark shadow at LockScreenCard.swift:102, and Settings → Card Style → Solid is a shipped escape. Worth wiring `preferDrawn: true` at the one call site (one line), but it is a fidelity risk on one opt-out surface, not something that stops use or trust.
- [CONFIRMED] (major/M) Increase Contrast is read, published, and then consumed by exactly two strokeBorder calls — it never reaches the panel, the compact island, or any lock-card content
    today: Turning on Increase Contrast changes exactly one thing in the app: the rim of the lyrics sync pill and the two lock-card panes goes from a lit gradient to a flat white 0.85 stroke. The tab rail, the segmented controls, the scrubber, every 1px hairline, every secondary/tertiary label, the compact island's hover state and the lock card's lyric column are byte-for-byte identical.
    apple: On macOS, Increase Contrast raises `separatorColor`, pushes `secondaryLabelColor` and `tertiaryLabelColor` toward the primary, adds a defined 1px border to every control that was relying on a fill to separate it, and removes decorative gradients. Every AppKit and SwiftUI system control does this without the app asking. The HIG treats it as a legibility requirement, not a style.
    impact: A user who turns on Increase Contrast because they cannot read low-contrast UI gets no relief from this app at all: the 0.46-white tab titles stay 0.46, the 0.18-white lyric lines stay 1.5:1, and the selected-tab chip stays invisible. The setting appears to do nothing, which reads as the app ignoring an accessibility preference — because it is.
    evidence: `rg 'increaseContrast' Sources` returns five lines: three in SystemAppearance.swift (read/publish/refresh) and two in GlassSurface.swift — line 267 (`.white.opacity(appearance.increaseContrast ? 0.85 : 0.18)`, the Reduce-Transparency-only `solid()` border) and line 298 (`shape.strokeBorder(.white.opacity(0.85), lineWidth: 1.5)` in `drawn()`). Nothing else in 66 source files reads it. The main panel is `NotchShape(...).fill(Color.black)` (NotchContentView.swift:75) with no border and no appearance observation; the compact island's only hover feedback is `liftFill` = white 0.015→0.075 (NotchContentView.swift:301-305); the rail chip is `Theme.surfaceHover` (NotchContentView.swift:577); the lock card's own type is `.white.opacity(0.62)` / `.white.opacity(0.45)` (LockScreenCard.swift:163, 285) and its lyric context lines are `.white.opacity(0.18)` (LyricRow.swift:78). CLAUDE.md claims "SystemAppearance watches Reduce Transparency and Increase Contrast and both are honored live."
    fix: Give `Theme` contrast-aware accessors instead of `static let` constants: `Theme.tertiary(_ a: SystemAppearance)` returning 0.46 / 0.75, `secondary` 0.55 / 0.88, `hairline` 0.10 / 0.40, `surface` 0.08 / 0.20, `surfaceHover` 0.14 / 0.34, and have `LyricRow` clamp its `distance` falloff floor from 0.18 to ~0.45 when contrast is on. Inject `SystemAppearance.shared` once at `NotchContentView` via an `@EnvironmentObject` so every pane picks it up, and add an outline to the compact island (a 1px white 0.5 stroke on the NotchShape) when contrast is on. Extend AccessibilityDisplayTests.swift to assert the token values actually change.
    challenges: Factually exact: `rg increaseContrast Sources` returns five lines — SystemAppearance.swift:21/29/57 and GlassSurface.swift:267-268 (the Reduce-Transparency-only `solid()` border) and :298 (`drawn()` rim). Nothing in the other 65 files reads it; the panel is `NotchShape(...).fill(Color.black)` with no border (NotchContentView.swift:75), the rail chip is `Theme.surfaceHover`/`Theme.surface` (NotchContentView.swift:576-579), the lock card's own labels are white 0.62/0.45 (LockScreenCard.swift:163). Reach is in fact even narrower than claimed: on macOS 26 without Reduce Transparency, the lock card and output picker take `glassEffect`, so line 298 fires only for the lyrics pill (`samplesBackdrop: false`, LyricsStage.swift:336). But severity is inflated: nothing becomes unreadable when Increase Contrast is on — every text token is already computed against black (Theme.swift:83-87, 4.58:1) and the bigger accessibility lever, Reduce Transparency, *is* honored end to end (GlassSurface.swift:233-240). The setting fails to *improve* marginal affordances; it does not degrade anything. | corrected: minor — a real accessibility gap plus a documentation overreach in CLAUDE.md ('both are honored live'), affecting the subset of users who enable Increase Contrast, none of whom lose access to any function. The user-visible symptom is the same low-contrast chrome everyone already sees (see selection-surfaces claim).
- [DISPUTED] (major/M) Every selection and affordance surface in the panel sits between 1.1:1 and 1.5:1 against its own background — the state is carried entirely by glyph brightness
    today: Which tab is active, which segment is selected, how long the track is, and where the artwork ends are all communicated by fills that are effectively invisible as shapes. Only the glyph/label brightness (white vs `Theme.tertiary` 0.46) actually carries the state.
    apple: macOS sidebars and segmented controls give the selected item an unmistakable fill — accent-tinted in a key window, a clearly lighter grey otherwise — and the floor for a UI component that conveys state (HIG / WCAG 1.4.11) is 3:1 against its adjacent background. Apple's own Now Playing scrubber track is a visible light rail, not a 1.1:1 whisper.
    impact: On a MacBook screen at typical brightness the user cannot see which of the five tabs is selected without reading the icon's brightness, cannot see the extent of the scrubber track to know how far through a song they are, and in Settings cannot see which of Glass/Solid is chosen. On a dimmed display or with any glare it becomes guesswork.
    evidence: Theme.swift:88-90: `surface = Color.white.opacity(0.08)`, `surfaceHover = Color.white.opacity(0.14)`, `hairline = Color.white.opacity(0.10)`. Over the panel's `Color.black`: 0.08 → 1.14:1, 0.10 → 1.20:1, 0.14 → 1.35:1. NotchContentView.swift:576-579 makes `surfaceHover` the *selected* tab and `surface` the *hovered* one — a 6% white difference, 1.18:1 chip-against-track. SettingsPane.swift:450-463 repeats it: the selected segment is `surfaceHover` inside a `surface` track. MediaPane.swift:234 makes the scrubber's unfilled track `Theme.surface` (1.14:1). MediaPane.swift:202-205 outlines the 118pt cover with `Theme.hairline` (1.20:1). The file that computed text contrast by hand (Theme.swift:83-87, "0.46 gives 4.58:1") never did the same arithmetic for non-text.
    fix: Raise the token floor: `surface` 0.08 → 0.12, `surfaceHover` 0.14 → 0.22, `hairline` 0.10 → 0.16, and separate *selected* from *hovered* by more than one step — give the selected rail chip its own token (white 0.24, ≈2.3:1) plus a 0.5pt white-0.35 `strokeBorder`, which is what ShelfPane.swift:157 already does correctly for a selected card. For the scrubber, 0.18 white gets the track to 1.54:1 with the filled portion at 0.9 doing the real work. Do this in the same edit as the Increase Contrast tokens above.
    challenges: Every named instance and every number checks out: Theme.swift:88-90 (surface 0.08, surfaceHover 0.14, hairline 0.10 → 1.14:1, 1.35:1, 1.20:1 over black, and 1.18:1 chip-against-track); NotchContentView.swift:576-579 makes surfaceHover the selected tab and surface the hovered one; SettingsPane.swift:450-463 repeats it (selected segment surfaceHover in a surface track); MediaPane.swift:234 `Capsule().fill(Theme.surface)` for the unfilled scrubber; MediaPane.swift:202-205 outlines the 118pt cover with Theme.hairline; Theme.swift:82-87 does the arithmetic for text (0.46 → 4.58:1, verified) and nowhere for a fill. But the universal quantifier is false. ShelfPane.swift:153 fills a selected tile with `Color.white.opacity(0.18)` (≈1.54:1) and ShelfPane.swift:76 draws the drag-drop target ring at `Color.white.opacity(0.6)` (≈7.4:1); MediaPane.swift:238-239 fills the elapsed scrubber at white 0.9. Progress and drop-targeting are carried by fills that are plainly visible. | corrected: The panel's *chrome* selection surfaces — active tab, selected segment, scrubber trough, artwork edge — all sit between 1.14:1 and 1.35:1 against black, so state there is carried almost entirely by glyph brightness (white vs Theme.tertiary 0.46). Theme.swift computed a contrast floor for text and never for a fill. The Shelf's selected tile (white 0.18) and drop-target ring (white 0.6), and the scrubber's filled portion (white 0.9), are exceptions above that band. ;; The arithmetic and every cited line are right — Theme.swift:88-90 defines surface 0.08 / surfaceHover 0.14 / hairline 0.10, which over `Color.black` (NotchContentView.swift:75) compute to 1.14:1, 1.35:1 and 1.20:1; NotchContentView.swift:577-578 makes surfaceHover selected and surface hovered (1.18:1 between them); SettingsPane.swift:451-462 puts a surfaceHover segment in a surface track; MediaPane.swift:234 is the empty scrubber track; MediaPane.swift:204 outlines the 118pt cover in hairline; and Theme.swift:83-87 does the contrast arithmetic for text and nowhere else. But the universal quantifier is false. | corrected: Accurate version: the app's three white-alpha surface tokens all land between 1.14:1 and 1.35:1 over the panel's black, and the states they carry — the selected tab chip, the Settings segmented selection, the empty scrubber track, the artwork outline — are effectively invisible as shapes, leaving glyph brightness (white vs Theme.tertiary 0.46) and, for the rail, the pane change below to do the work. Not 'every selection and affordance surface': the switch at SettingsPane.swift:414 is a native `.switch` on the system accent, the sliders at SettingsPane.swift:67 and :95 are tinted Theme.secondary (white 0.55 ≈ 6.3:1), the scrubber's filled portion is white 0.9 (MediaPane.swift:238), the selected shelf tile adds a white 0.55 1.5pt border and a checkmark badge on top of a 1.54:1 fill (ShelfPane.swift:153-175), and the lock card's selected output uses `NSColor.controlAccentColor` (LockScreenCard.swift:427). Note too that Apple's own dark-mode selection fills (unemphasized list selection, segmented control) sit around 1.8–2.5:1, not the 3:1 WCAG 1.4.11 asks — so the gap is real but narrower than 'nothing is visible'. ;; Numbers check out: Theme.swift:88-90 (`surface` 0.08, `surfaceHover` 0.14, `hairline` 0.10 white on the panel's `Color.black`), NotchContentView.swift:576-579 makes the *selected* tab `surfaceHover` and the *hovered* one `surface` (a 6% delta), SettingsPane.swift:450-463 repeats the pattern, MediaPane.swift:234 fills the unplayed scrubber track with `Theme.surface`. That is a genuine WCAG 1.4.11 (3:1 non-text) failure. But one supporting example is simply wrong — 'how long the track is' *is* stated in text at MediaPane.swift:303 (`Text(formatTime(media.duration))`) — and the claim's own concession undercuts it: selection is carried by white vs `Theme.tertiary` 0.46 glyphs, by a 1.15 hover scale (NotchContentView.swift:566), and by `.isSelected` accessibility traits (NotchContentView.swift:559, SettingsPane.swift:466). No user is left unable to tell which tab or segment is active. | corrected: minor — non-text contrast fails the 3:1 bar and the selection pills read as invisible shapes, which is a craft/accessibility gap; it is not a state-legibility failure, because the label and glyph brightness carry the state unambiguously. The strongest sub-case is the 4pt scrubber track at 1.14:1 (its seekability is not advertised until hover).
- [DISPUTED] (major/L) The expanded panel body is a hardcoded black slab; the app's entire material system never touches it, and unlike the lock card it offers no Glass/Solid choice
    today: Clicking the notch expands a 560×208 opaque black rectangle over the desktop. The `.card` elevation, the specular streak, the grain, the rim, the `tint:`/`light:` parameters and Apple's `glassEffect` all exist and are all unreachable from the surface the user spends 99% of their time on.
    apple: Black is correct where the panel abuts hardware — NotchContentView.swift:490-494 is right that the wings must be zero-light. But Apple's own surfaces that expand *past* the menu bar over the desktop — Control Center, Notification Center, a menu-bar extra's panel — are all NSVisualEffectView/Liquid Glass sampling the wallpaper. On macOS 26 every one of them became Liquid Glass.
    impact: The panel reads as an overlay pasted on the desktop rather than a system surface set into it, and there is no way for a user who wants it to match Control Center to get that. Every paid competitor (NotchNook, Alcove, DynamicLake Pro) ships a translucency option; this is a visible reason to prefer them at a glance.
    evidence: NotchContentView.swift:71-76 is the whole panel background: `NotchShape(topRadius:bottomRadius:).fill(Color.black).frame(width: size.width + 2 * topRadius, height: size.height)`. `rg 'glassSurface\(' Sources` finds three call sites, none in the panel. `rg 'Material|ultraThin' Sources` finds one hit outside GlassSurface — LockScreenCard.swift:116. SettingsPane.swift:170 ships `Card Style` (Glass/Solid) for the *lock card* only; there is no equivalent for the panel. `VibrancyBackdrop` (the app's `NSVisualEffectView` wrapper) is instantiated in exactly one place, GlassSurface.swift:69, which the panel never reaches.
    fix: Split the shell background at the notch line: keep `Color.black` for the first `geometry.notchSize.height` points (the band abutting the cutout, which must stay zero-light) and give the body below it `glassSurface(cornerRadius:elevation: .card, samplesBackdrop: true)` clipped to the lower part of the `NotchShape`. Gate it behind a `Panel Style` choiceRow in SettingsPane mirroring the existing `Card Style` at line 170, defaulting to Solid so nothing changes for current users. This also finally exercises `Elevation.card`, the `light:` parameter and `usesSystemGlass` on the surface they were written for.
    challenges: Every code fact holds: NotchContentView.swift:71-76 is the entire panel background (`NotchShape(...).fill(Color.black).frame(...)`), the three `glassSurface(` call sites are LyricsStage.swift:336 and LockScreenCard.swift:105/:381 with none in the panel, `VibrancyBackdrop` is instantiated only at GlassSurface.swift:69, and the Glass/Solid choice at SettingsPane.swift:167-181 is lock-card-only. Two problems. (a) The grep evidence is wrong — `rg 'Material|ultraThin' Sources` also hits VibrancyBackdrop.swift:6 and :16 (`NSVisualEffectView.Material`) and LyricsStage.swift:27/41/42 (`MaterializeModifier`), not 'one hit outside GlassSurface'. (b) The nativeness argument inverts Apple's own precedent. | corrected: Accurate version: the panel body is a hardcoded opaque black slab and the app's elevation/rim/grain/tint vocabulary is unreachable from it. But that is a defensible choice, not a material defect: Apple's Dynamic Island is opaque black in every iOS release including 26 — it did not become Liquid Glass — precisely so the software panel fuses with the hardware cutout, and on a Mac the notch is a physical black cutout, so a translucent body hanging off it would break the very continuity the shape exists to sell. GlassSurface.swift:4-10 scopes itself to 'every translucent surface here'; the panel was never one. The defensible gap is narrower: inside the panel there is no depth vocabulary at all — its sub-surfaces are flat white-alpha fills with no elevation, no rim and no shadow token — and there is no user control over panel opacity for people who want the desktop to show through. ;; The facts hold: NotchContentView.swift:71-76 is `NotchShape(...).fill(Color.black)`, the three `glassSurface(` call sites are LockScreenCard.swift:105, :381 and LyricsStage.swift:336, and `VibrancyBackdrop` is instantiated only at GlassSurface.swift:69. But the severity assumes the panel *should* carry material, and opaque black is the correct choice here, not an oversight: the panel hangs off the physical display cutout (NotchShape's concave shoulders exist to melt into it, NotchShape.swift:3-5) and any translucency would show wallpaper through a shape whose whole illusion is being a continuation of the black cutout — the same reason every competitor draws a black body. The comment at NotchContentView.swift:315-319 shows the team already reasoned about matching cutout black. Unreached `tint:`/`light:` parameters are dead API, not a user-facing defect; and because the body is already opaque, Reduce Transparency needs no special handling there. | corrected: polish — an open design question ('should the part of the body below the menu-bar line carry a material on macOS 26?'), not a defect. No user complains that the notch panel is black; they would complain if it were see-through.
- [DISPUTED] (minor/M) Seven shadow call sites, eight distinct hand-tuned values, no ambient+key pair — and the panel's shadow bleeds 18pt onto the menu bar
    today: Every raised thing in the app invents its own shadow. The open panel gets a single `0.5 / r18 / y8` — one blob, no split between a tight contact shadow and a wide ambient one — and that blob darkens the menu bar for 18pt to the left and right of the panel while it is open. LockScreenCard.swift:124-130 reached the opposite conclusion for the card and removed its shadows entirely ("a card floating on a drawn shadow reads as a sticker laid on the wallpaper rather than a pane set into it"), but the same reasoning was never applied to the panel.
    apple: A macOS window shadow is two shadows: a broad ambient one (large radius, low alpha, little or no offset) and a tight key/contact one just under the leading edge. That pair is what makes a window read as *above* the desktop rather than as a dark rectangle. Apple also never lets a floating panel cast onto the menu bar.
    impact: Over a bright wallpaper the open panel has a visible hard grey halo and a darkened smear over the menu bar items nearest it; over a dark wallpaper it has no perceptible separation from the desktop at all. Neither state reads like a macOS window.
    evidence: `rg '\.shadow\(' Sources` → NotchContentView.swift:77-81 (`color: .black.opacity(isOpen ? 0.5 : (compactActivity.isVisible ? 0.28 : 0)), radius: isOpen ? 18 : 5, y: isOpen ? 8 : 2`), MediaPane.swift:206 (`0.5, r12, y5`), MediaPane.swift:246 (`0.4, r3`), LockScreenCard.swift:102 (`0.55, r4, y1`), :190 (`0.45, r10, y4`), :220 (`0.4, r2, y1`), :382 (`0.35, r18, y8`). Theme.swift has no shadow token at all, while `GlassSurface.Elevation` (GlassSurface.swift:20-53) defines rim, base and grain per elevation and stops short of shadow. The panel shape's top edge is flush with `screen.frame.maxY` (NotchGeometry.swift:222-229, `windowPadding.top = 0`), so an 18pt-radius shadow spreads 18pt horizontally into the menu bar strip either side of the panel's shoulders.
    fix: Add `Theme.shadow(for: GlassSurface.Elevation)` returning an (ambient, key) pair — card: ambient `black 0.30 / r36 / y12` + key `black 0.22 / r6 / y2`; popover: `0.24 / r22 / y8` + `0.18 / r4 / y1`; pill: `0.20 / r10 / y3` — applied via two chained `.shadow` modifiers, and replace all seven call sites. For the panel, kill the horizontal bleed by casting from a shape whose top edge starts at `notchSize.height` rather than 0, so nothing falls on the menu bar.
    challenges: The audit is exact: `rg '\.shadow\(' Sources` returns precisely the seven sites listed (NotchContentView.swift:77, MediaPane.swift:206 and :246, LockScreenCard.swift:102, :190, :220, :382), NotchContentView.swift:77-81 carries two of the eight distinct values in one ternary, Theme.swift defines no shadow token while GlassSurface.Elevation (GlassSurface.swift:20-53) stops at rim/base/grain, and LockScreenCard.swift:124-130 does argue the opposite case for the card. The menu-bar bleed also checks out mechanically: windowPadding is `top: 0, left: 40, right: 40` (NotchGeometry.swift:76), the window is 700pt wide against a 560pt default body (NotchMetrics.swift:13/25), windowFrame is flush with `screen.frame.maxY` (NotchGeometry.swift:222-229), and the panel sits at statusWindow+1, above the menu bar — so an r18/y8 shadow has room to spread sideways over the menu bar strip and nothing clips it. What is wrong is the standard being applied. | corrected: Accurate version: seven shadow call sites, eight hand-tuned values, no shadow token in Theme and none in GlassSurface.Elevation — so 'raised' has no shared definition, and the open panel's r18/y8 blob does darken the menu bar strip either side of its shoulders. Drop the 'no ambient+key pair' framing: paired ambient+key shadows are Material Design's elevation model, not Apple's. macOS composites a single window-server shadow per window, AppKit and SwiftUI ship no two-layer shadow convention, and the HIG defines no shadow elevation scale — LockScreenCard.swift:124-130's conclusion (let the material define its own edge, draw no shadow) is closer to what Apple actually does than a split contact/ambient pair would be. ;; Confirmed inventory: `rg '\.shadow\(' Sources` returns exactly the seven sites listed (NotchContentView.swift:77, MediaPane.swift:206 and :246, LockScreenCard.swift:102, :190, :220, :382), the panel site branching into two values, and Theme.swift carries no shadow token. The menu-bar bleed is geometrically real but weaker than described: NotchGeometry.swift:76 gives the window `left/right: 40`, `top: 0`, so the shadow is clipped above and its `y: 8` downward offset pushes the blob away from the strip; what lands on the menu bar is the faint upper tail of an 18pt blur beside a panel that is itself black, over a strip that is already dark. The lock card's shadow-free reasoning (LockScreenCard.swift:124-130) is about a card floating free on a wallpaper — the panel is anchored to the screen edge, a different case, so 'the same reasoning was never applied' is not obviously an error. | corrected: polish — missing shadow tokens and an ambient/key split are internal-consistency craft; the menu-bar tail is faint and unnamed by users. Worth folding into Theme when elevation tokens are next touched.
- [CONFIRMED] (polish/M) The panel silhouette is drawn with quadratic Béziers while every other rounded shape in the app — and the platform — uses continuous corners
    today: The panel's bottom corners have a curvature discontinuity at the tangent points — the arc starts and stops abruptly where a continuous corner eases in. Next to a real macOS window corner (or next to the app's own artwork tile at MediaPane.swift:193) the difference in corner personality is visible at 22pt.
    apple: Apple has used continuous (squircle) corners on every window, sheet, popover, control and hardware display corner since macOS 11. The MacBook Pro's physical notch fillets are continuous curves, not circular arcs and not parabolas.
    impact: The open panel's bottom corners read very slightly "cheaper" than the windows around them — the kind of thing users register as "not quite right" without naming it. On a non-notch display the synthetic pill's proportions also drift from a real cutout's, so the illusion is weakest on exactly the machines where it is doing all the work.
    evidence: NotchShape.swift:26-44 builds all four corners with `path.addQuadCurve(to:control:)`. Every other rounded shape in the codebase is explicitly continuous: `RoundedRectangle(cornerRadius:, style: .continuous)` at GlassSurface.swift:259, 282, MediaPane.swift:193, 201, 203, ClipboardPane.swift:88, SettingsPane.swift:397, 451, 462, TranslatePane.swift:111, NotchContentView.swift:371, 553. The open bottom radius is 22 (Theme.swift:79), where a quadratic corner is measurably tighter — a quad from (0,r) to (r,0) with the control at the corner passes through (0.25r, 0.25r), i.e. 1.06r from the corner centre versus r for a circular arc and ~0.93r for a squircle, so the silhouette differs by roughly 2.5pt at that radius. The synthetic notch on non-notch displays is a hardcoded `CGSize(width: 180, height: max(menuBarHeight, NSStatusBar.system.thickness, 24))` (NotchGeometry.swift:187) using the same 6/9 radii as the physical one, so its aspect drifts from ~6.2:1 (real 14"/16" cutout) to 7.5:1 at a 24pt menu bar and 4.7:1 at 38pt.
    fix: Replace the four `addQuadCurve` calls in NotchShape.swift with cubic segments matching the continuous-corner parameterisation (control points at ~0.55·r and ~0.83·r along each leg — the superellipse basis `RoundedRectangle(style: .continuous)` uses), keeping `animatableData` as-is so the open/close interpolation is unchanged. Separately, derive the synthetic notch width from its height (`height * 6.2`, clamped 160…220) rather than a fixed 180, so the drawn pill holds a real cutout's proportions on any menu-bar height.
- [CONFIRMED] (polish/S) The grain tile is a 96px bitmap given a 96pt NSImage size, so on every Retina display it is smoothly upscaled 2× — the noise becomes soft 2×2 blocks rather than per-pixel dither
    today: The comment at GlassSurface.swift:124-126 calls the grain "the single mark that stops the surface reading as a flat fill — worth more than any amount of tuning the gradients above it." On the Retina displays this app exclusively runs on, that mark is a blurred low-frequency mottle at 2–3.5% opacity, not the high-frequency dither it was written to be.
    apple: Apple's materials (and the noise layer inside NSVisualEffectView) dither at native device resolution — the point of the noise is to break up banding at the pixel level, which a 2×-smoothed tile cannot do.
    impact: On a large surface like the 460×300 lock card in its drawn form, the gradient can band visibly over a gradient wallpaper, because the anti-banding noise has been low-pass filtered away. The surface reads flatter than the recipe intends — exactly the failure the grain was added to prevent.
    evidence: GlassSurface.swift:143-174 builds the tile with `NSBitmapImageRep(pixelsWide: side, pixelsHigh: side, ...)` at `side = 96` (line 139) and then `let image = NSImage(size: NSSize(width: side, height: side))` — 96 pixels at 96 points, i.e. 1× backing. GlassSurface.swift:127-131 draws it as `Image(nsImage: Self.grain).resizable(resizingMode: .tile).opacity(elevation.grain).blendMode(.overlay)` with no `.interpolation(.none)`, so SwiftUI's default smooth interpolation resamples it to 192 device pixels on a 2× display.
    fix: Set the image's point size to half its pixel size on 2× displays: after building the rep, `rep.size = NSSize(width: side / 2, height: side / 2)` and `image.size = rep.size` (or generate at `side * backingScaleFactor` and set `image.size` to `side`), and add `.interpolation(.none)` at GlassSurface.swift:128 so nothing resamples it in either direction. `grainTile(side:seed:)` already takes `side`, so the deterministic-tile test stays valid.
- [CONFIRMED] (minor/M) The `tint:` and `light:` parameters that let artwork colour a surface are dead — the media pane, the compact island and the lock card are all achromatic
    today: Open the panel to Music and the surface is the same black whether the album is a black-and-white portrait or a saturated neon cover. The machinery to make the panel carry the record's colour exists, is documented, and is switched off everywhere except one pane.
    apple: Apple lights surfaces from artwork where the surface is *content*, not chrome: the Music app's Now Playing background, tvOS shelves, and the iOS lock-screen player's blurred cover wash. It does not tint controls — which is why LockScreenCard.swift:72-81's refusal to accent the transport is right and should stay.
    impact: Nothing is broken, but the panel is visually inert next to Alcove and NotchNook, whose artwork bloom is the single most-screenshotted thing about them. This is the cheapest available answer to "why use this over the $25 app" — and unlike them, the code already has the restraint boundary drawn in the right place (chrome stays white, only the substrate takes colour).
    evidence: `glassSurface(...)` accepts `tint: Color?` (GlassSurface.swift:198) and `light: NSImage?` (199), and `GlassSurface` implements both: a weak tint gradient at lines 101-107 and a 52pt-blurred artwork wash at lines 72-83, plus a macOS 26 equivalent at line 278 (`Glass.regular.tint(tint.opacity(0.22))`). None of the three call sites passes either. The only place artwork lights anything is LyricsStage.swift:154-171 (`Image(nsImage: artwork).blur(radius: 60).opacity(0.35)`), which hand-rolls it rather than using `light:`. MediaPane's cover is artwork + `Theme.hairline` + a shadow on flat black (MediaPane.swift:192-207). LockScreenCard.swift:108-110 rejects a cover tint deliberately and correctly.
    fix: Wire the existing parameter through: pass `light: vm.media.artwork` from the Music pane's background so the panel body carries a blurred wash at the `.card` elevation's already-tuned 0.28 opacity / 0.72 saturation (GlassSurface.swift:78-80), which was chosen to sit under white type. Gate it behind the panel-material setting from the finding above so it only applies when the body is glass, and leave the compact island and every control untouched. Delete `tint:` if it stays unused rather than leaving a live parameter with no caller.
    challenges: Dead-parameter claim verified: `glassSurface(...)` declares `tint:` (GlassSurface.swift:198) and `light:` (:199), `GlassSurface` implements both (lines 72-83 artwork wash, 101-107 tint gradient, 278 the macOS 26 `Glass.regular.tint(...)`), and grepping `tint:|light:` outside GlassSurface.swift returns nothing — no call site passes either. LyricsStage.swift:154-171 hand-rolls its own blurred artwork wash instead. But the user-visible framing is weak: the claim itself concedes LockScreenCard.swift:108-110 rejects a cover tint 'deliberately and correctly' (with the same reasoning as LockScreenCard.swift:71-79 on accents: 'Apple's own player does not do this'), and the media pane's black body is dictated by cutout continuity, not by a missing tint. So this is unused API plus one duplicated implementation, and separately a possible *feature* (artwork-lit surfaces are a competitor differentiator) — not a defect users experience. | corrected: polish — dead parameters and a duplicated artwork-wash implementation in LyricsStage; the achromatic surfaces are a defended design choice, so any change here is a new feature decision, not a fix.

## typography
verdict: Family choice is clean and deliberate — SF Pro everywhere via `.system(size:weight:)`, zero `design: .rounded`, zero custom faces, so optical sizing (SF Pro Text↔Display at 20pt) happens automatically and correctly. Monospaced digits are genuinely well handled: every ticking number (scrubber, lock-card times, tab counters, lyric offset, slider readouts) already carries `.monospacedDigit()`, and there is no volume readout to jitter — restraint, not an omission. Colour contrast was reasoned about with real arithmetic (Theme.swift:81-90). Where it falls short of Apple-native is not taste, it is metrics: the app's one typographic abstraction (`islandFont` / `Theme.tracking(forSize:)`) rests on a premise that is measurably false on macOS and reaches only 7 of ~87 text sites; there is no type scale behind those sites (15 distinct text sizes, four of them half-point); nothing marquees, so long track titles die in an ellipsis on all three surfaces; the elapsed/remaining labels are pinned to a 32pt gutter and an m:ss format, so anything over 99 minutes renders literally as `120:…`; and there is no text-size accommodation at all, defended by a "the window cannot grow" argument the geometry contradicts by 192pt. None of this is unfixable — the bones are good and most of it is S/M work — but as shipped, one podcast plus one long title is enough to make the panel look like it was measured for a single three-minute song.
- [CONFIRMED] (major/S) Elapsed/remaining time renders as "120:…" for anything over 99 minutes — there is no hours format and the gutter is 32pt
    today: Play a two-hour podcast, DJ set, audiobook chapter or long YouTube tab — all of which publish to Now Playing and all of which this app reads — and both ends of the scrubber collapse to `120:…` and `145:…`. The seconds are gone and the position is unreadable. The lock card escapes truncation (no fixed frame) but still shows `120:45` / `-145:07`, a duration format macOS uses nowhere.
    apple: Music, Podcasts, QuickTime and Control Center's Now Playing module all roll into `h:mm:ss` past sixty minutes (`2:00:45`) and widen the time gutter to fit — Podcasts shows `1:35:12` / `-24:48`, sized from the item's real duration rather than an assumed maximum. Nothing in Apple's media UI ever truncates a timecode.
    impact: For every non-song item — podcasts, mixes, audiobooks, long video — the scrubber stops telling you where you are. It is not subtle degradation: the label is three digits and an ellipsis. A user who lives in Podcasts sees this on every single episode.
    evidence: `Sources/IslaKit/UI/Theme.swift:121-125` — `func formatTime(_ seconds:) -> String { … return String(format: "%d:%02d", total / 60, total % 60) }` — minutes only, never rolls into hours. Duplicated verbatim at `Sources/IslaKit/UI/LockScreenCard.swift:665-669` (same body, different fallback string). The gutters are fixed: `Sources/IslaKit/UI/MediaPane.swift:226` `.frame(width: 32, alignment: .leading)` and `MediaPane.swift:304` `.frame(width: 32, alignment: .trailing)`, both at `MediaPane.swift:306` `.font(.system(size: 10, weight: .medium).monospacedDigit())`. The stale comment at `MediaPane.swift:310-312` states the assumption outright: "formatTime yields 'm:ss' through 'mm:ss' in the 10 pt monospaced ramp". Measured with NSAttributedString at that exact font: "59:59" = 29.54pt (fits), "120:45" = 36.11pt (does not). Rendered through SwiftUI's ImageRenderer at scale 4 into the real 32pt frame, the rightmost ink lands at 30.25pt and the drawn string is `120:…` — matching the measured width of "120:…" (31.69pt), not "120:4…" (38.26pt). Confirmed visually: a rendered scrubber row reads `120:…  ————  145:…`.
    fix: One shared formatter replacing both copies. In `Theme.swift`, make `formatTime` roll hours: `let h = total/3600; return h > 0 ? String(format: "%d:%02d:%02d", h, (total%3600)/60, total%60) : String(format: "%d:%02d", total/60, total%60)`. Delete `LockScreenCard.formatTime` (665-669) and call the shared one — the design doc's own "one timeline, one renderer" rule applies to timecodes too. Then size the gutter from content instead of pinning it: replace the two `.frame(width: 32)` in `MediaPane.scrubber` with a computed `timeGutter` (`media.duration >= 3600 ? 52 : 32`). Add a test beside `ScrubPreviewTests` asserting `formatTime(7245) == "2:00:45"` and that the rendered label never exceeds its gutter for durations up to six hours.
    challenges: Facts hold exactly. Sources/IslaKit/UI/Theme.swift:120-124 `String(format: "%d:%02d", total / 60, total % 60)` never rolls into hours, duplicated at Sources/IslaKit/UI/LockScreenCard.swift:665-669 (and the lock card's right label is `"-" + formatTime(...)` at LockScreenCard.swift:499). Gutters are fixed 32pt at MediaPane.swift:226 and :304 with `.font(.system(size: 10, weight: .medium).monospacedDigit())` at :306. I re-measured with NSAttributedString at that exact font: "59:59" = 29.54pt (fits), "120:45" = 36.11pt (does not), "120:…" = 31.69pt — so the truncation is real above 99:59. But severity is inflated: it only bites media longer than 99 minutes, the scrubber bar itself still conveys position, nothing breaks, and no one abandons or distrusts the app over it. It is a bug a podcast/audiobook listener files, not one they complain loudly about. | corrected: minor — wrong duration format (no h:mm:ss) plus tail truncation in a 32pt gutter, visible only on media over 99 minutes; the bar still shows position and nothing else degrades.
- [CONFIRMED] (major/M) No marquee anywhere — long track titles tail-truncate on the peek, the open pane and the lock card
    today: The peek — the moment the app exists for, the two-second glance at a new track — shows about eighteen characters and then an ellipsis, while the pill is drawn 128pt wider than the content it truncates. In the open pane at the narrow end of the width slider, much of a normal music library truncates.
    apple: The iPhone Dynamic Island marquees the track title in both its compact and expanded Now Playing presentations. On macOS, Control Center's Now Playing module and Music's LCD both scroll an overflowing title, pausing at each end. Apple's rule is that a title too long to fit is shown over time, not cut — an ellipsis on a song title is for lists, not for the one-item now-playing surface. Every paid competitor in this category scrolls the title.
    impact: You cannot tell which track just started from the peek unless its name is short, and two tracks from the same album peek identically. This is the single most visible thing separating this from a $25 app people already bought.
    evidence: `rg -n "marquee|Marquee" Sources/IslaKit` returns nothing. Every title is `.lineLimit(1)` with default tail truncation: peek title `Sources/IslaKit/UI/NotchContentView.swift:192-195` (`.font(.system(size: 10, weight: .semibold))`, `.lineLimit(1)`), open pane title `Sources/IslaKit/UI/MediaPane.swift:110-113` (`.islandFont(16, weight: .semibold)`, `.lineLimit(1)`), lock card title `Sources/IslaKit/UI/LockScreenCard.swift:157-160` (`.islandFont(18, …)`, `.lineLimit(1)`). Measured columns: the peek's left wing is `wingWidth = (size.width - notchSize.width)/2` (`NotchContentView.swift:206`), and with `NotchMetrics.sneakPeekExtension = 300` (`NotchMetrics.swift:63`) on a ~190pt notch that is 150pt, minus `.padding(.leading, 10)` (`NotchContentView.swift:205`), 22pt artwork and 7pt spacing = **111pt for the title**. Measured at 10pt semibold: "Everything In Its Right Place" = 141.1pt, "The Sound of Silence (Acoustic Version)" = 201.4pt, a Russian classical title = 284.8pt — all truncate. The open pane column is 352pt at the 560 default and **272pt at the 480 minimum** (`NotchMetrics.minimumBodyWidth`), where "Bohemian Rhapsody - Remastered 2011" (293.5pt at 16pt semibold) truncates.
