This is the most-filed complaint against every app in this category, which is why NotchNook, Alcove and Boring Notch all ship a "hide in fullscreen" toggle.
    evidence: `collectionBehavior = [.canJoinAllSpaces, .stationary, .fullScreenAuxiliary, .ignoresCycle]` at `NotchPanel.normalLevel` = `CGWindowLevelForKey(.statusWindow) + 1` (NotchPanel.swift:36-38 and 146). `NotchController.install()` observes exactly screen parameters, active space, screens-sleep and screens-wake plus lock/unlock (NotchController.swift:47-122); nothing observes `NSWorkspace.didActivateApplicationNotification` and nothing ever reads `NSWorkspace.shared.frontmostApplication` or any fullscreen state. `sneakPeek()` guards on open/drop-target/locked/pointer only (NotchController.swift:1018-1028). There is no user setting — no fullscreen key exists in NotchViewModel.swift:231-367.
    fix: Observe `NSWorkspace.didActivateApplicationNotification` in `install()` alongside the existing four observers, and on each activation determine whether the frontmost app owns a fullscreen window on the notch's display (compare `CGWindowListCopyWindowInfo` bounds against `screen.frame`, combined with the space change already observed at NotchController.swift:54-60). Add a `hideInFullScreen` default — on by default for synthetic notches, off for physical ones where the notch band is letterboxed black anyway — that calls the existing `setOpen(false)` plus `panel.orderOut(nil)`, and restores with `orderFrontRegardless()`, which `activeSpaceChanged()` already does (NotchController.swift:155).
- [CONFIRMED] (minor/S) Launching an already-running Isla does nothing visible, and two copies can run at once
    today: Double-clicking Isla.app while it is already running produces no Dock bounce (no Dock icon), no window, no panel and no menu-bar flash — literally no feedback. First launch is the same: the app starts, an unlabeled pill appears at the notch, and nothing says what it is or that ⌥⌘I exists. Two copies at different paths (say `/Applications` and `~/Downloads`) each get a panel, each spawn a media helper, and the second's hotkey registration fails silently per the finding above.
    apple: Apple's accessory apps answer a reopen: menu-bar extras flash or open their menu, and `applicationShouldHandleReopen(_:hasVisibleWindows:)` exists precisely so a windowless app can show something. macOS's own first-run experience for a background utility is a one-time panel explaining where it lives.
    impact: A user who forgets whether Isla is running has no way to find out and no feedback when they try to start it — the classic "did it install?" moment, made worse by there being no Dock icon and no menu-bar item by design. Duplicate instances produce two overlapping islands with no explanation.
    evidence: `AppDelegate` implements only `application(_:open:)`, `applicationDidFinishLaunching` and `applicationWillTerminate` (AppDelegate.swift:11, 17, 83); `rg -n "applicationShouldHandleReopen" Sources/` returns nothing. `IslaApplication.run()` sets the delegate, the `.accessory` policy and calls `app.run()` with no `NSRunningApplication.runningApplications(withBundleIdentifier:)` check (IslaApplication.swift:14-21). There is no first-run state: `rg -n "firstRun|onboard|hasLaunched|welcome" Sources/` returns nothing.
    fix: Implement `applicationShouldHandleReopen(_:hasVisibleWindows:)` in `AppDelegate` calling `controller?.toggle()` — a reopen from Finder is exactly the request "show me the app." Add a single-instance guard at the top of `IslaApplication.run()`: if another running app shares `ProductIdentity.bundleIdentifier`, post a distributed notification the live instance answers by toggling, then exit. Add a one-time first-run peek using the `sneakPeek()` machinery that already exists (NotchController.swift:1018-1045) so the very first launch shows the user what the island does and where Settings is.
- [CONFIRMED] (polish/S) Info.plist metadata reads as unfinished: copyright is "MIT License", no app category, build number equals marketing version
    today: About Isla shows "Isla / Version 0.1.0 / MIT License" — a license identifier where the copyright line belongs, with no author, no year and no credits. Finder's Get Info shows no category, and any future updater comparing `CFBundleVersion` has no monotonic build number to compare.
    apple: Apple's own apps put a real copyright sentence in `NSHumanReadableCopyright`, declare `LSApplicationCategoryType`, and keep `CFBundleVersion` monotonic and separate from the marketing version. The About panel is product surface, not a plist formality — here it is the only place the app's identity is ever stated to the user.
    impact: The one dialog in the app that says who made it says nothing. A user who opens About to check what they installed — reasonable, for an unsigned app they just dequarantined — sees a bare license word with no way to reach a site, a repo or an author.
    evidence: `<key>NSHumanReadableCopyright</key><string>MIT License</string>` (Scripts/bundle.sh:72) — that is the string macOS puts on the copyright line of the standard About panel, which `SettingsPane` opens via `NSApp.orderFrontStandardAboutPanel(nil)` (SettingsPane.swift:245). `CFBundleVersion` and `CFBundleShortVersionString` are both `$VERSION` (Scripts/bundle.sh:63-64). There is no `LSApplicationCategoryType` and no `CFBundleGetInfoString` in the plist (Scripts/bundle.sh:36-74), and `Scripts/test-identity.sh` checks four keys without covering these.
    fix: In the Scripts/bundle.sh plist heredoc, set `NSHumanReadableCopyright` to a real copyright line, add `LSApplicationCategoryType` (`public.app-category.utilities`), and derive `CFBundleVersion` from a separate monotonic build counter in `Scripts/version`. Extend `Scripts/test-identity.sh`, which already asserts four keys, to cover them so they cannot regress. Optionally call `orderFrontStandardAboutPanel(options:)` with a `.credits` `NSAttributedString` linking the repo and the MIT notice already shipped at `Contents/Resources/Licenses/`.
    challenges: The two substantive halves are confirmed. Scripts/bundle.sh:72 is `<key>NSHumanReadableCopyright</key><string>MIT License</string>`, and that key is exactly what AppKit renders on the copyright line of the panel SettingsPane.swift:245 opens via orderFrontStandardAboutPanel — with no Credits.rtf anywhere (Resources/ holds only AppIcon.icns, Isla.entitlements, en.lproj, ru.lproj), so About really does read 'Isla / Version 0.1.0 / MIT License' with no author, year or credits. CFBundleVersion and CFBundleShortVersionString are both $VERSION (Scripts/bundle.sh:63-64), so there is no monotonic build number, and Scripts/test-identity.sh:10-13 covers four keys without touching either. But the Finder claim is wrong about what Apple's software does: LSApplicationCategoryType is documented as 'the category that best describes your app for the App Store' and is consumed by the App Store, Spotlight (kMDItemAppStoreCategory) and Screen Time — Finder's Get Info panel does not display an app category, so its absence is invisible there. CFBundleGetInfoString is also a poor ask: it is a legacy key from the classic Get Info window that modern macOS ignores in favor of NSHumanReadableCopyright and CFBundleShortVersionString. | corrected: Info.plist has two real metadata defects: NSHumanReadableCopyright is the license identifier 'MIT License' rather than a copyright line (Scripts/bundle.sh:72), which is what the standard About panel prints (SettingsPane.swift:245), and there is no Credits.rtf; and CFBundleVersion equals CFBundleShortVersionString (Scripts/bundle.sh:63-64), leaving no monotonic build number for a future updater. LSApplicationCategoryType is also absent, which costs App Store/Spotlight/Screen Time categorization — not a Finder Get Info row. CFBundleGetInfoString is a legacy key modern macOS ignores and is not a gap.

## media-fidelity
verdict: The core clock is the best part of this app and beats every paid competitor I know of: the anchor/rate extrapolator (MediaController.swift:1117), the staleness guard for MediaRemote's re-served elapsed/timestamp pair (`describesAMomentAlreadyPast`, :951), the phantom-trajectory seek verdict (`judgeSeekReading`, :110), the rewind-corroboration rule (`corroboratesRewind`, :1057), the Spotify precision loop with half-round-trip latency compensation (:452), and the flag-vs-rate settle window (ReportedPlayback.swift:24) are all correct, all motivated by a named bug, and all tested. The transport path is equally strong — per-PID player paths and per-PID command caches with pid-recycling eviction (helper.m:149), `commands: nil` meaning unknown rather than none (NowPlayingFeed.swift:32), a real watchdog for the silent-but-alive helper (:144), NDJSON resync after an oversized record, and an artwork re-request verb so a lost cover is recoverable. Position accuracy, app-switch flicker, artwork latency and rate handling are all genuinely solved — I could not find drift over a long track, and the artwork blanking delay at :735 is a level of care almost nobody ships. What is not Apple-native is everything at the edges of that core: the app speaks only four MediaRemote commands out of the vocabulary sessions actually advertise (so podcasts get two dead arrows and browser videos get a scrubber that rubber-bands), it fires Apple Events on the primary path with the panel closed — contradicting its own entitlement contract in three documents — and when the media route dies it degrades silently and permanently, with the user seeing only "Nothing is playing". Fix the command vocabulary, the failure surface and the consent hygiene and this dimension is better than anything on sale.
- [CONFIRMED] (major/S) The scrubber is always draggable, even when the session never advertised seek — the bar rubber-bands back seconds later
    today: Drag the scrubber on a Safari/Chrome video session or any player that registers no seek handler: `seek(to:)` calls `setAnchor(clamped)` (MediaController.swift:503) so the bar jumps to the drop point, `pendingSeek` is armed, the command is sent and dropped by the daemon without a word, and 1.5s later `judgeSeekReading` returns `.settleAdopt` on the unmoved reading. That reading is a large backward delta, so `adopt` holds it as a `rewindCandidate` and waits for a second reading to agree (MediaController.swift:1090-1101). Net effect: the bar sits at the target for roughly 3-4 seconds and then snaps back to where the track really is.
    apple: Control Center's Now Playing tile renders a static progress bar, not a draggable one, for sessions that do not advertise `SeekToPlaybackPosition`. The HIG rule is that a control which cannot act is either absent or visibly disabled — never live and silently ineffective. This app already applies exactly that rule to skip (`.disabled(!media.canSkip)` plus 0.35 opacity, MediaPane.swift:337-338), so the standard is internally established and just not extended to seek.
    impact: On browser video — the single biggest reason to install a notch app that reads MediaRemote rather than scripting Music — dragging the position bar looks like it worked and then visibly undoes itself. It reads as the app being broken, and it is the worst kind of broken because it is intermittent by source. The lyrics feature inherits it: clicking a lyric line on a non-seekable session bounces the song back.
    evidence: `NowPlayingFeed.Command` has only four cases — `case play = 0, pause = 1, next = 4, previous = 5` (Sources/IslaKit/Services/NowPlayingFeed.swift:45) — so `snapshot.offers(.seek)` cannot even be asked. MediaController publishes `canSkip` from `snapshot.offers(.next) && snapshot.offers(.previous)` (MediaController.swift:635) but no `canSeek` exists anywhere (`rg -n 'canSeek' Sources` → no matches). MediaPane draws the scrubber on duration alone — `if media.duration > 0 { scrubber }` (MediaPane.swift:140) — and its `DragGesture.onEnded` calls `media.seek(...)` unconditionally (MediaPane.swift:265-276). Same on the lock card (LockScreenCard.swift:487-489) and from every lyric click. The helper already reports the seek code: `MRCommandSeekToPlaybackPosition = 24` (helper.m:74) and `refreshCommands` puts *every* enabled code into `commands` (helper.m:169-185), so the data is already on the wire and simply unread.
    fix: Add `case seek = 24` to `NowPlayingFeed.Command` (NowPlayingFeed.swift:44). Publish `@Published private(set) var canSeek = true` on MediaController, set it in `apply` beside `canSkip` (`let seekable = snapshot.offers(.seek)`, :635), and reset it to true in `clear()` and `switchToScriptingFallback()` alongside `canSkip` (:834, :858). In MediaPane render the filled capsule without `.gesture`/`.onHover`/`.accessibilityAdjustableAction` when `!media.canSeek`, and do the same in LockScreenCard's `seekBar`. Guard the lyric-click seek in LyricsStage the same way (fall back to scroll-only). Add a MediaControllerTests case asserting `canSeek == false` for a snapshot whose `commands` set omits 24 and `true` when `commands` is nil.
- [CONFIRMED] (major/M) The app scripts Spotify on every track change with the panel closed and lyrics off, triggering the automation consent prompt out of nowhere
    today: First launch, Spotify already playing, panel never opened, lyrics off: the next track change fires an Apple Event at Spotify and macOS puts up "Isla wants access to control Spotify" with no panel on screen and no user action that could explain it. The answer is fetched for a feature that is switched off, and the retry ladder fires it up to five times per track (:386-391).
    apple: Apple's own Now Playing surfaces never ask for anything — MediaRemote is the whole point. Where Apple software does need Apple Events it asks at the moment of the user's own action, and the usage string describes truthfully what will happen. A TCC dialog raised by an `.accessory` app with no window, no Dock icon and no menu-bar item is the definition of an unexplained prompt: there is nothing on screen the user can attribute it to.
    impact: A surprise system permission dialog attributed to an app with no visible presence is what makes people uninstall — and for an unsigned direct-download DMG it reads as malware behaviour. It also burns the one-shot consent: deny it and Spotify precision sync, shuffle/repeat and word-synced lyrics are permanently dead with no in-app path to recover.
    evidence: `apply` calls `requestSpotifyTrackID(for:playerPID:attempt:)` on every `trackChanged || playerChanged` (MediaController.swift:594). That method guards only `displayedPlayerIsSpotify` (:375) — no `isActive` guard, no lyrics guard — and sends a real Apple Event via `PlayerBridge.spotifyTrackID` (PlayerBridge.swift:64-78). `NotchViewModel.showLyricsEnabled` is `UserDefaults.standard.bool(forKey:)` with no registered default, i.e. off by default (NotchViewModel.swift:250-252), and `spotifyTrackID` is consumed only by lyric loading and the lock-card heart (`rg -n spotifyTrackID Sources`). `refreshPlaybackModes()` (:293) and `precisionCorrect()` → `preciseSpotifyPosition` (:422) also run on the primary MediaRemote path. Meanwhile the Info.plist string says "controls Music or Spotify when its primary media route is unavailable" (Scripts/bundle.sh:70-71), the entitlements file says consent "is requested by macOS at the moment the scripting fallback first drives Music or Spotify" (Resources/Isla.entitlements:5-6), and checklist.md:27-28 repeats it. All three are contradicted by the code.
    fix: Gate every Apple Event behind actual need: add `guard isActive else { return }` and a lyrics/lock-card enablement check to `requestSpotifyTrackID` (MediaController.swift:375), and re-issue the lookup from `setActive(true)` when the id is nil for the displayed track so the panel still gets it on first open. Then either (a) correct `NSAppleEventsUsageDescription` in Scripts/bundle.sh:70 to state the real uses — "reads Spotify's exact playback position and track id for synced lyrics, and controls Music or Spotify when the system media route is unavailable" — and amend Resources/Isla.entitlements:4-6 and checklist.md:27-28 to match, or (b) keep the narrow contract and move precision sync + track id behind an explicit "Sync lyrics precisely with Spotify" toggle in the Music section of SettingsPane, default off, per the project's own rule that anything touching another app defaults to off.
    challenges: Mechanism confirmed: MediaController.swift:594 calls requestSpotifyTrackID on every `trackChanged || playerChanged`, and :375 guards only `displayedPlayerIsSpotify` — no isActive, no lyrics check — reaching PlayerBridge.spotifyTrackID (PlayerBridge.swift:64-78), with the ladder capped at `attempt < 4` i.e. five sends. But the two other cited call sites are in fact guarded: refreshPlaybackModes is called as `if isActive { refreshPlaybackModes() }` (:600) and precisionCorrect only runs when `isActive && isPlaying && displayedPlayerIsSpotify` (:401). The Info.plist string also already says 'reads the current track' (Scripts/bundle.sh:71), so only the entitlements comment (Resources/Isla.entitlements:5-6) and checklist.md:27-28 are actually contradicted. User-visible cost is a one-time TCC prompt at a moment the user did not trigger, plus a per-track AppleScript into Spotify forever; a denial then degrades silently (PlayerBridge.swift:390-391 only NSLogs). Annoying and wasteful, not something that stops use or trust. | corrected: minor — an unexplained one-time automation prompt plus a per-track Apple Event fired for features that are switched off; the docs/entitlement comment is wrong about when consent is asked.
- [CONFIRMED] (major/M) When the MediaRemote route dies the app says "Nothing is playing" — the same words it uses when nothing is playing
    today: macOS 16 closes the perl loophole, or a point release renames `MRMediaRemoteGetNowPlayingInfo`. The helper reports it honestly, the app falls back, and if the user listens on YouTube or Podcasts the Music tab reads "Nothing is playing" with two "Open Spotify / Open Music" buttons — while music is audibly playing.
    apple: macOS never confuses "no content" with "cannot read content": Control Center hides the Now Playing module entirely when there is no session, and system UI that has lost a capability states the condition. The HIG's empty-state rule is that the message must distinguish absence from failure — a distinction this app's own comment at MediaPane.swift:452-453 claims to be making and does not.
    impact: On the day a macOS update breaks the private route, every user concludes the app is broken and uninstalls rather than checking for an update. There is no diagnostic anyone can report back, and with no Dock icon or menu-bar item there is nowhere else to look.
    evidence: `switchToScriptingFallback()` logs and nothing else: `NSLog("Isla: Now Playing helper unavailable, falling back to Music/Spotify scripting")` (MediaController.swift:859). `feedAvailable` is private (:48) and no state describing the media route is `@Published`. When the fallback finds no scriptable player running, `PlayerBridge.currentState` completes nil and `clear()` runs (:875), so `media.track == nil` and MediaPane draws `emptyState` — `Text(localized("Nothing is playing"))` (MediaPane.swift:454). Resources/en.lproj carries exactly one relevant string (line 37) and none for a degraded route. The helper does distinguish the case — it emits `{"error": "mediaremote-symbols-missing"}` precisely so a macOS release that renames a symbol is visible (helper.m:265) — and NowPlayingFeed turns it into `.unavailable` (NowPlayingFeed.swift:358-364), where the information is then thrown away.
    fix: Add `enum MediaRoute { case system, scriptedOnly, unavailable }` as `@Published private(set) var route` on MediaController, set to `.scriptedOnly` in `switchToScriptingFallback()` (:854) and `.unavailable` when the fallback also has no candidates (`refreshFromPlayers`, :875, when `PlayerApp.allCases.filter(\.isRunning)` is empty). In MediaPane's `emptyState`, branch on it: `.system` keeps today's copy; `.scriptedOnly` reads "Only Music and Spotify can be read on this Mac" with the existing Open buttons; `.unavailable` reads "Now Playing is not available on this version of macOS" plus a Check for Updates affordance. Add both keys to Resources/en.lproj and ru.lproj (test-localizations.sh enforces parity). Mirror the `.scriptedOnly` note into the Music section of SettingsPane so it is discoverable when the panel is closed.
    challenges: Facts hold: switchToScriptingFallback only NSLogs (MediaController.swift:859), `feedAvailable` is private (:48) and nothing about the route is @Published; helper.m:265 emits `mediaremote-symbols-missing` and NowPlayingFeed.swift:358-364 turns it into stop()+onUnavailable, discarding the distinction; refreshFromPlayers → clear() (:875) leaves track nil so MediaPane.swift:97/447-454 draws 'Nothing is playing', the only relevant string in en.lproj (line 37). But this is the messaging half of a failure that today is rare (it requires the helper route to die, i.e. the same event as the one-way-fallback claim), and the wrong copy is a diagnosability/trust gap rather than a loss of function on its own. Note MediaPane.swift:453 even comments that the string is 'status, not instruction' — the state it cannot express is exactly the degraded route. | corrected: minor — no honest copy for a degraded media route, so a rare failure reads as 'nothing is playing'; the functional loss belongs to the one-way-fallback finding.
- [CONFIRMED] (major/S) One bad minute at login costs the good media path until the app is quit — and there is no easy way to quit and relaunch
    today: At login, with the machine thrashing, three 12-second silences in a row — or three quick helper deaths while the disk is busy — permanently demote the app to scripting Music and Spotify for the rest of that session. Browser video, Podcasts, VLC, IINA, everything non-scriptable disappears from the island and never comes back.
    apple: Apple's own daemon-backed clients (Control Center, the menu-bar Now Playing item) reconnect to `mediaremoted` when it comes back; XPC invalidation handlers re-establish rather than latch. Nothing in macOS treats a transient daemon failure as permanent for the process lifetime.
    impact: The app's headline capability — reading anything that plays, not just two apps — silently and irreversibly turns off, and because the app deliberately has no Dock icon, no menu-bar item and no window, the user has no obvious way to restart it. The failure is invisible (see the previous finding), so they will not know to.
    evidence: `feedAvailable` is set false in `switchToScriptingFallback()` (MediaController.swift:854) and is only ever set back to true in `stop()` (:233), called from `NotchStores.stop()` (NotchStores.swift:64) from `NotchController.teardown()` (NotchController.swift:440) — i.e. app termination. Nothing calls `feed.start()` again. `NowPlayingFailurePolicy` reaches `.fallback` after only three consecutive failures (`guard consecutiveFailures < 3`, NowPlayingFailurePolicy.swift:44), and a failure is recorded by the watchdog for mere silence of 12s (NowPlayingFeed.swift:83, :164-181) as well as for a crash. `recordSuccess()` (:54) resets only the consecutive counter and is never consulted after the fallback is taken.
    fix: Give the fallback an exit. In `switchToScriptingFallback()` schedule a probe (`DispatchQueue.main.asyncAfter(deadline: .now() + 300)`) that calls a new `retrySystemRoute()`: tear down the scripting observers (the same lines as :243-244), set `feedAvailable = true`, reset `failurePolicy` to a fresh `NowPlayingFailurePolicy()` and call `feed.start()`. Back the probe off (5 min → 15 → 60, capped) and skip it entirely when the last failure was `.unavailable` from the helper's `mediaremote-symbols-missing` payload, which is a real capability loss rather than a hiccup. Also probe once on `NSWorkspace.screensDidWakeNotification`, which NotchController already observes (NotchController.swift:82). Cover it with a NowPlayingLifecycleTests case that drives three failures, advances the clock, and asserts the feed restarts.
- [DISPUTED] (major/M) Podcast sessions get two dead grey arrows instead of the ⏪15 / ⏩30 that Control Center shows for the same session
    today: Play an episode in Apple Podcasts, Overcast or Pocket Casts. Artwork and title are correct, the scrubber works, and the two transport arrows are visibly greyed out with no substitute — the dimming convention correctly reports "this session cannot skip tracks" and then offers nothing the session can do.
    apple: Control Center's Now Playing tile and the Lock Screen player swap the next/previous glyphs for `gobackward.15` / `goforward.30` when the session registers `MPSkipIntervalCommand` instead of next/previous track, and use the session's own `preferredIntervals` for the number inside the glyph. It is one of the most recognisable pieces of Apple Now Playing behaviour.
    impact: Podcasts are the second-biggest use of a Now Playing surface after music, and skipping an ad is the most common podcast action. Today the island is strictly worse than reaching for the app — and worse than the free Boring Notch, which does offer skip. It also makes the app look like it does not understand what it is showing.
    evidence: The command vocabulary is four codes: `case play = 0, pause = 1, next = 4, previous = 5` (NowPlayingFeed.swift:45). A session that advertises skip-forward/backward but not next/previous therefore fails `snapshot.offers(.next) && snapshot.offers(.previous)` (MediaController.swift:635), `canSkip` goes false, and both arrows are disabled at 0.35 opacity with nothing offered in their place (MediaPane.swift:333-354, LockScreenCard.swift:549-565). The plumbing to fix this already exists end to end: `refreshCommands` collects every enabled `command` code from the session's info dictionaries into `commands` (helper.m:169-185), and `handleCommand` parses `cmd <code> <pid>` with a plain `parts[1].intValue` cast (helper.m:331-334), so any code the app names is already deliverable without touching the dylib.
    fix: Extend `NowPlayingFeed.Command` with the skip codes read off a live podcast session's `GetSupportedCommandsForPlayer` (the same method the file's comment at NowPlayingFeed.swift:39-43 documents for the four current codes — read them, do not assume the old global enum). Carry the interval through: have `refreshCommands` also copy each info dict's `preferredIntervals` into the payload (helper.m:172-179) so the glyph can say 15 or 30 rather than guessing. Publish `@Published private(set) var skipInterval: (back: Int, forward: Int)?` next to `canSkip`, and in MediaPane's `controls` (MediaPane.swift:333-354) render `gobackward.\(back)` / `goforward.\(forward)` in the same two slots when it is non-nil, keeping the five-slot grammar intact. Mirror it in LockScreenCard:549-565 and add localized accessibility labels ("Skip Back 15 Seconds") to both .lproj tables.
    challenges: The code gap is real: the command vocabulary is four codes (NowPlayingFeed.swift:45), `canSkip = snapshot.offers(.next) && snapshot.offers(.previous)` (MediaController.swift:635), and both arrows are `.disabled(!media.canSkip).opacity(media.canSkip ? 1 : 0.35)` with no substitute (MediaPane.swift:333-352, LockScreenCard.swift:544-564). macOS is also correctly described — MPRemoteCommandCenter's skipForward/skipBackward with preferred intervals exist on macOS and the system Now Playing UI mirrors advertised commands, so this is not an iOS-only affordance. But two details are wrong. First, the symptom is conditional, not the default: `offers` returns true when `commands` is nil (NowPlayingFeed.swift:32-34), so the arrows only go grey when the helper successfully reads a command set that lacks 4 and 5 — podcast clients that also expose queue next/previous keep working arrows and show no dimming at all. Second, the fix is not purely Swift-side: `handleCommand`'s `cmd` verb sends `sendCommandToPlayer(command, nil, playerPID)` with nil options (helper.m:333-336), whereas an interval skip needs an options dictionary the way seek does (`kMRMediaRemoteOptionPlaybackPosition`, helper.m:351), so "already deliverable without touching the dylib" is not established. | corrected: Isla has no vocabulary for skip-interval commands — its four codes (play/pause/next/previous) cannot express skip-forward/backward — so a session that advertises only skip intervals gets next/previous arrows dimmed to 0.35 with nothing offered in their place, while macOS's own Now Playing UI renders interval-skip glyphs for the same session. The greyed-arrow symptom appears only when the helper actually reports a command set without codes 4/5 (nil `commands` leaves the arrows enabled), and delivering a skip command would also require adding an options dictionary to the helper's `cmd` path, as `seek` already does. ;; The certain part is confirmed: the vocabulary is four codes (NowPlayingFeed.swift:45), nothing anywhere names skip-forward/backward, and helper.m:169-185 / :331-334 already carry every enabled code so no dylib change is needed. The 'two dead grey arrows' premise is not verified — it holds only for a session that advertises neither next(4) nor previous(5) (MediaController.swift:635, dimmed at MediaPane.swift:333-354); a podcast app that does advertise them shows live arrows that skip episodes instead of 15/30s, which is a smaller gap. Either way the transport is honest about its limit (unlike the seek finding) and play/pause plus the scrubber still work, so this is a missing capability versus Control Center, not a broken or lying control. | corrected: minor — no 15/30s skip for podcast sessions, so at best the arrows skip episodes and at worst they dim with nothing offered in their place.
- [CONFIRMED] (major/L) When two players are live, a 10-second timer is the only arbitration and the user cannot override it
    today: Spotify playing, then a paused YouTube tab is focused in Chrome. The island keeps Spotify for exactly ten seconds — with its state frozen, so a pause pressed in Spotify during that window does not register — and then flips to the paused video, with no way to flip it back except focusing Spotify again.
    apple: Apple ships the escape hatch this app lacks: iOS/iPadOS Control Center stacks every live Now Playing session and lets you tap between them, and macOS's menu-bar Now Playing item follows the session actually producing audio rather than focus. Apple's model is heuristic plus an explicit override, not heuristic alone — and the heuristic here is a good one, better than following focus blindly.
    impact: Anyone who keeps a browser open while listening to music (i.e. everyone) sees the island periodically show the wrong thing with no way to correct it. This is the most common complaint about every app in this category and the most defensible reason to charge for one.
    evidence: `apply` holds the display against a non-playing stranger for `foreignHoldWindow: TimeInterval = 10` (MediaController.swift:57, :556-568), and during the hold it `return`s before applying anything — playing state, position, artwork and commands all freeze. Past the window the stranger is adopted with no user input. There is no UI or setting for choosing a session: SettingsPane's Music section has only Show Lyrics, Peek at New Tracks, Show on Lock Screen, Card Style and Clear Lyrics Cache (SettingsPane.swift:132-190). The list of live sessions is one line away — the helper already dlsyms and calls `MRMediaRemoteGetNowPlayingClients` and throws the answer out: `if (sGetClients) sGetClients(sQueue, ^(NSArray *clients) {});` (helper.m:386).
    fix: Consume the clients list already being fetched. In helper.m:386 keep the array, map each client to `{pid, bundleID, title, artist, isPlaying}` and publish it as a `clients` array (or on a separate `{"clients": [...]}` line so `emitPayload`'s dedupe still works on the main payload). Decode it into `NowPlayingFeed.Snapshot.clients` (NowPlayingPayloadDecoder.swift:44-46 is the pattern), publish `@Published private(set) var sessions: [Session]`, and add `private var pinnedPID: pid_t?` consulted at the top of `apply` (:556) so a pinned session is never displaced — cleared when its process dies, which NowPlayingFeed already observes (:106-113). Surface it by making the source label at NotchContentView.swift:400 a menu of live sessions with their `NSRunningApplication` icons, which `refreshSourceIcon` already resolves (:764-774). Record it in checklist.md as a capability addition.
- [CONFIRMED] (minor/S) A stalled or buffering session keeps the bar (and the lyric) running, at whatever rate the last player reported
    today: A network stall mid-track (`playing: true, rate: 0`) leaves the clock free-running at the last positive rate for the whole stall, corrected in a lurch by the next snapshot up to 2s later. Switch from a 2× podcast straight to a session that reports `rate: 0` while playing and the bar advances at 2× real time until a positive rate arrives.
    apple: Control Center's scrubber and the Lock Screen player stop when the session's rate is 0 — the elapsed time freezes and resumes when audio does. Apple treats `MPNowPlayingInfoPropertyPlaybackRate == 0` as authoritative about motion even while the session remains "playing".
    impact: On a flaky connection the progress bar drifts ahead of the audio and snaps back every couple of seconds. For the lyrics feature — the app's differentiator — the highlighted line runs ahead of the voice during a buffer and then jumps back, which is the exact stutter the elaborate precision-sync work (:452-467) exists to eliminate everywhere else.
    evidence: `if snapshot.rate > 0 { playbackRate = snapshot.rate }` (MediaController.swift:663) — a reported rate of 0 is discarded rather than treated as a stall, and `ReportedPlayback.resolve` still returns true from the `playing` flag (ReportedPlayback.swift:32). `tick()` then extrapolates unconditionally: `anchor.position + Date().timeIntervalSince(anchor.at) * playbackRate` (:1119). `playbackRate` is also not reset when the player changes — the `if playerChanged` block at :596-609 resets `positionSettled`, `shuffleEnabled`, `repeatMode`, `reportedPlayback` and `lastReadingAt`, but not the rate — and the only reset is in `clear()` (:835), which runs only for an empty snapshot.
    fix: Treat rate 0 as a stall rather than as no news. Track `private var isStalled = false`, set from `snapshot.rate == 0 && reportedPlaying` in `apply` beside the existing rate line (:663), and have `tick()` (:1117) return early while stalled so the anchor holds the position instead of extrapolating. Reset `playbackRate = 1` inside the `if playerChanged` block (:596-609) alongside the other per-player state, so no session ever inherits another's speed. Add a MediaControllerTests case: apply a 2× snapshot, apply a `playerChanged` snapshot with `rate: 0, playing: true`, advance the clock, assert the position did not move.
- [CONFIRMED] (minor/M) The output picker exists — but only on the lock screen, where the Mac is locked
    today: To move audio to AirPods or a HomePod, or to change output volume from the island, the user must lock the Mac. Unlocked, the island can start, stop and seek the music but cannot say where it goes.
    apple: Every Apple Now Playing surface carries the route control: Control Center's tile has the AirPlay glyph in its corner, the Lock Screen player has it under the scrubber, and the menu-bar Now Playing item has it inline. Route selection is treated as part of Now Playing, not as a separate feature.
    impact: The capability is already built, tested and localized — it is simply unreachable in the state the user is in 99% of the time. Against MediaMate and DynamicLake Pro, whose output/volume HUD is a headline feature, this is a visible hole in a product that already has the parts.
    evidence: `AudioOutputs.available()/current()/select()` and `SystemVolume` are referenced from exactly one view: LockScreenCard.swift:415-419, :457-459, :527-530 (`rg -n 'AudioOutputs|SystemVolume' Sources` returns nothing outside those two service files and LockScreenCard). MediaPane's `controls` row is shuffle / previous / play / next / repeat (MediaPane.swift:323-367) with no route or volume affordance, and the trailing header slot for the Music tab is an equalizer plus the source name (NotchContentView.swift:397-401).
    fix: Lift LockScreenCard's `outputPicker` (LockScreenCard.swift:~340-459) and `volumeBar` (:507+) into a shared `OutputPicker`/`VolumeBar` view in Sources/IslaKit/UI, parameterised by tint (both should use `controlAccentColor`, per the rationale already written at LockScreenCard.swift:331-339). Add a route glyph — `airplayaudio`, or `AudioOutputs.current()`'s own symbol, for which AudioOutputSymbolTests already exists — to the Music tab's trailing header slot in NotchContentView.swift:397-401, presenting the picker as an overlay over MediaPane the way the card presents it over the player. Keep `Haptics.levelChange` on selection so both surfaces feel identical, and note the panel-side capability in checklist.md beside the existing lock-card entry.
- [DISPUTED] (minor/S) A live stream drops the scrubber and with it every trace of time — no elapsed counter, no "Live"
    today: Play a radio stream, a YouTube live broadcast or a browser video whose duration MediaRemote does not report. The pane shows artwork, title, artist, an empty lyric slot and the transport, with a silent vertical gap where the scrubber was and no indication of how long it has been playing or that its length is unknowable.
    apple: Control Center and the Lock Screen player replace the scrubber with a "LIVE" label for a live session, and show a running elapsed counter for content of unknown duration rather than nothing. The state is named, not blanked.
    impact: The pane reads as half-loaded rather than correct. It is also the one case where the app shows less than the source app, which is a bad look on exactly the browser-video sessions the private media route exists to reach.
    evidence: MediaPane renders the whole time row only when a duration exists: `if media.duration > 0 { scrubber }` (MediaPane.swift:140), and the elapsed and total labels live inside that view (:224, :303). The lock card behaves the same way, with `fraction` returning 0 when `media.duration <= 0` (LockScreenCard.swift:466-467) and its drag guarded on `media.duration > 0` (:483, :487). Nothing anywhere treats `duration == 0` as a named state: `rg -n 'live|isLive' Sources` returns nothing.
    fix: In MediaPane, replace the bare `if media.duration > 0` (MediaPane.swift:140) with an else branch that keeps the same row height and renders `formatTime(media.position)` on the left plus a `localized("Live")` capsule on the right — the position clock is already correct for these sessions, since `reportedPosition` returns the aged reading unclamped when `duration <= 0` (:930). Do the same in LockScreenCard's `seekBar`, and add "Live" to both .lproj tables. This also removes the layout hole, since the row then keeps its slot the way `captionHeight` already does for the lyric line (:44).
    challenges: The user-visible behavior checks out: MediaPane.swift:140-142 renders the scrubber only `if media.duration > 0`, both time labels live inside it (MediaPane.swift:224, 303), the surrounding `Spacer(minLength: 6)`/`Spacer(minLength: 4)` inside a fixed `.frame(height: blockHeight)` (MediaPane.swift:135-146) leave the space empty rather than collapsing it, and the lock card matches (`fraction` returns 0 when duration <= 0, LockScreenCard.swift:464-467; drags guarded at :483/:487). But the cited evidence is false: `rg -n 'live' Sources -g '*.swift'` returns many matches, including MediaPane.swift:137 — "A live stream has no duration, and a scrubber with no length is a control that reads 0:00 / 0:00, refuses to be dragged, and looks broken rather than absent." So the case was considered and the hiding is a deliberate, documented decision, not an unnoticed hole; what is missing is the substitute (an elapsed counter or a LIVE marker), which is what Apple's own surfaces show for durationless streams. | corrected: For a durationless session Isla hides the entire time row, leaving a reserved but empty band with no elapsed counter and no LIVE marker (MediaPane.swift:140-142, LockScreenCard.swift:464-467). Hiding the scrubber is an explicit design decision documented at MediaPane.swift:137 — a 0:00/0:00 bar reads as broken — so the gap is the absent substitute, not an unconsidered `duration == 0` path. ;; Verified: MediaPane.swift:140 gates the whole time row on `media.duration > 0` (the elapsed/total labels live inside it at :224 and :303), LockScreenCard.swift:464-467 returns fraction 0 and :483-489 guards the drag the same way, and no state is named for duration==0. But the omission is deliberate and documented at MediaPane.swift:137-139 ('a scrubber with no length … looks broken rather than absent'), and the 'silent vertical gap' is inaccurate — the surrounding Spacer(minLength: 6)/Spacer(minLength: 4) inside the fixed blockHeight absorb the space. Nothing is wrong on screen; what is missing is an elapsed counter and a 'Live' label that a radio listener would rarely name. | corrected: polish — no 'Live' treatment or elapsed counter for durationless sessions; the layout itself degrades cleanly.
- [CONFIRMED] (polish/M) Nothing takes any colour from the cover — defensible on the lock card, less so in the panel
    today: Every track looks identical: the same glass, the same white type, the same neutral shadow, regardless of what is playing.
    apple: Genuinely split, and the code has the macOS half right. Control Center's Now Playing tile does not tint from artwork, and the argument at LockScreenCard.swift:108 — that real glass should sample the wallpaper, not the album — is exactly Apple's Liquid Glass reasoning. But Apple's own Music app does derive colour from artwork for its full-screen player and Now Playing background on both macOS and iOS. So the restraint is correct for the lock card and arguably a missed opportunity in the panel, where the surface belongs to the app rather than to the system.
    impact: Nothing is broken and no user will call this a bug. But it is the first thing anyone notices comparing screenshots against NotchNook or Alcove, both of which bloom the panel edge in the cover's colour, and it is a large part of why those apps feel alive and this one feels correct-but-cool.
    evidence: There is no colour extraction anywhere: `rg -n 'averageColor|dominant|CIAreaAverage' Sources` returns nothing, and `decodeArtwork` produces a plain NSImage with no analysis (MediaController.swift:779-798). The lock card states the decision explicitly — "No tint from the cover either. The glass takes its character from the wallpaper it is actually over, which is the point of it being glass" (LockScreenCard.swift:108-110) — and the output picker comment reinforces it by choosing `controlAccentColor` over an artwork tint (:331-339). MediaPane's artwork is a plain rounded rect with a hairline and a neutral black shadow (MediaPane.swift:202-206), and the pane's surface calls `glassSurface(...)` with no tint argument.
    fix: If taken at all, take it narrowly and leave the lock card exactly as it is. Extract a single average colour on the existing background decode pass (MediaController.swift:779, where `NSBitmapImageRep` is already in hand — a 1×1 CIAreaAverage draw costs microseconds), publish it as `@Published private(set) var artworkTint: Color?`, clamp its saturation and luminance so it can never fight the white type, and feed it only to MediaPane's own `glassSurface(...)` tint parameter (GlassSurface.swift:197 already accepts one and applies it at 0.14/0.02 drawn, 0.22 on real glass — all conservative). Cross-fade on `Theme.artworkAnimation` with the artwork so a skip does not flicker, and skip it entirely when SystemAppearance reports Increase Contrast. Record the decision as a dated amendment in docs/plans/2026-08-18-dynamic-island-parity-design.md rather than editing the lock card's existing rationale.

## performance
verdict: This is the strongest dimension of the app and it is close to Apple-native already — I measured it rather than guessed. A normally launched instance with the panel closed and nothing playing sits at 27.7 MB physical footprint (`vmmap -summary`: `Physical footprint: 27.7M / peak: 28.0M`), 0.33 % of one core (0.60 s of CPU accumulated over a 180 s window), 10 idle wake-ups in 60 s, and 0.34 s of CPU from launch to a drawn island. A 30 s `sample` of the idle process found every main-thread sample blocked in `mach_msg` and exactly one `+[NSEvent mouseLocation]` frame among ~8,600. The engineering behind that is deliberate: JPEG decode and TIFF→PNG transcode are off the main thread (MediaController.swift:777, ClipboardStore.swift:206), every `@Published` write on the 2 s media path is compared before it fires (MediaController.swift:627-635, 803), the equalizer is a Core Animation layer that pauses its own clock rather than a SwiftUI timeline (Skeleton.swift:105-122), the panel's content is structurally removed when collapsed so no pane's timeline survives a close (NotchContentView.swift:96), every collection is capped and pruned off-main (clipboard 40 × 256 KB, shelf 60, screenshot vault and lyric cache both LRU), every timer carries a tolerance so the kernel can coalesce it, and — contrary to the usual assumption about this app class — there is no always-on global event monitor: the only `addGlobalMonitorForEvents` is armed while the panel is pinned and removed on close (NotchController.swift:553, 568). The helper also dies cleanly with the app (verified: no orphan after `pkill`). What is left is four specific holes rather than a class of problem — the media helper never stands down when the screen is dark, the Shelf tab redoes all of its disk work on every visit and blocks the main actor doing it, nothing in the app knows Low Power Mode or thermal pressure exists, and the geometry watchdog beats forever. None of these lose a head-to-head on the Activity Monitor number alone, but the first two are what a reviewer with a clamshell MacBook or an iCloud-heavy Downloads folder will hit.
- [DISPUTED] (major/M) The MediaRemote helper polls every 2 s forever — nothing stands it down when the screen is dark, the Mac is locked, or nothing has played all night
    today: The perl helper and `mediaremoted` exchange a round trip every 2 seconds for the entire life of the app, and the app parses a JSON line every 2 seconds, regardless of whether the display is asleep, the Mac is locked, the lid is shut, or no media session has existed for hours. The sleep handler stops the pointer sampler, the clipboard poll and the position ticker — and leaves this running.
    apple: Apple's energy guidance is that a process does no discretionary work while the display is asleep; system agents register for `NSWorkspace.screensDidSleepNotification` and stand down — which is exactly what this controller already does for its other three polls. Apple's own now-playing consumers are notification-driven and back off when there is no session.
    impact: A MacBook left on the desk overnight with the lid shut — or simply display-asleep-but-awake, the ordinary state of an idle Mac — has two processes waking each other thirty times a minute until morning. In Activity Monitor's Energy tab one of them is named `perl`, which is also a trust problem: a user who finds it has no way to tell it belongs to the notch app.
    evidence: `Sources/IslaMediaHelper/helper.m:414` — `[NSTimer scheduledTimerWithTimeInterval:2.0 repeats:YES block:^(NSTimer *timer) { @autoreleasepool { publish(); } }];`, commented "Cheap enough at this interval to run unconditionally rather than gate it on whether anything is playing". The helper's command vocabulary (`handleCommand`, helper.m:303-340) understands only `get`, `art`, `cmd `, `seek ` — there is no pause verb. On the Swift side `NowPlayingFeed` has only `start()`/`stop()` (NowPlayingFeed.swift:89, 118) and no suspend path, and its silence watchdog fires every `silenceTimeout / 3` = 4 s (NowPlayingFeed.swift:83, 147). The one place that quietens the app for a dark screen does not touch it: `NotchStores.suspendForIdleScreen` is two lines — `clipboard.stop()` and `if !keepingMediaRunning { media.setActive(false) }` (NotchStores.swift:82-85) — and is all the `screensDidSleepNotification` handler calls (NotchController.swift:66-79). Measured with the panel closed and one paused session: the `perl` host accumulated 0.18 s of CPU over 180 s (0.10 % of a core) on top of Isla's own 0.60 s; each tick is an XPC round trip that also wakes `mediaremoted`, plus one NDJSON line parsed on the main actor (NowPlayingFeed.swift:330-337 → NowPlayingPayloadDecoder.swift:13).
    fix: Add `idle` and `resume` verbs to `handleCommand` in helper.m that invalidate and re-arm the 2 s `NSTimer` (it lives on the feed thread's run loop, so do it via `dispatch_async(sQueue, ...)` the same way `art` clears `sArtworkID`). Give `NowPlayingFeed` a `suspend()`/`resume()` pair that writes those lines, stops and restarts the silence watchdog, and issues a `refresh()` on resume. Call them from `NotchStores.suspendForIdleScreen`/`resumeFromIdleScreen` so the existing `screensDidSleep`/`screensDidWake` wiring at NotchController.swift:66-110 picks it up for free, plus the lock path when the lock card is off. Separately, back the helper's own interval off: after N consecutive identical publishes with no session, step 2 s → 10 s and snap back on the first MediaRemote notification or `get` — that keeps the macOS 26 workaround the comment documents while making an idle Mac cheap.
    challenges: The core is real: Sources/IslaMediaHelper/helper.m:414 schedules `[NSTimer scheduledTimerWithTimeInterval:2.0 repeats:YES ... publish()]` with the exact quoted comment, the command vocabulary is only get/art/cmd/seek (helper.m:308,310,331,346 — no pause verb), NowPlayingFeed exposes only start()/stop() (NowPlayingFeed.swift:91,118) with the silence watchdog at silenceTimeout/3 = 4 s (NowPlayingFeed.swift:83,147), and the sleep handler (NotchController.swift:66-79) calls only stores.suspendForIdleScreen(), which is exactly `clipboard.stop()` + `media.setActive(false)` (NotchStores.swift:82-85). MediaController.setActive(false) (MediaController.swift:254-286) stops the ticker/precision loop but never touches feed; feed.stop() is reached only from MediaController.stop() (MediaController.swift:241), i.e. app quit. I confirmed the perl child stays alive with the panel closed (ps showed `/usr/bin/perl ... libislamedia.dylib` as a child for the whole run) and dies with the app. What is exaggerated is the app-side cost: helper.m:45-52 and 93-113 dedupe identical payloads and repeat an unchanged one only on a 5 s heartbeat (kHeartbeatInterval = 5.0), so an idle session emits a line about every 6 s, not every 2 s. | corrected: The helper does a MediaRemote/XPC round trip every 2 s for the app's whole life with no gate on display sleep, lock or lid, and no suspend verb — but the app parses an NDJSON line only about every 6 s while idle (helper-side dedupe plus a 5 s heartbeat, helper.m:93-113), not every 2 s. ;; The core is right: Sources/IslaMediaHelper/helper.m:414 schedules an unconditional 2 s NSTimer -> publish() -> MRMediaRemoteGetNowPlayingInfo XPC round trip, handleCommand (helper.m:303-340) has get/art/cmd/seek and no pause verb, NowPlayingFeed has only start()/stop() (NowPlayingFeed.swift:89,118), and stop() is reached only from MediaController.stop() (MediaController.swift:241) <- NotchStores.stop() (NotchStores.swift:60), i.e. app quit. The screensDidSleep handler (NotchController.swift:66-79) calls pointer.stop() + stores.suspendForIdleScreen(), and suspendForIdleScreen (NotchStores.swift:82-85) only does clipboard.stop() + media.setActive(false); setActive(false) (MediaController.swift:254-286) merely tears down the 0.25 s position ticker (updateTicker, :1098-1115) and never touches the feed. So the helper and mediaremoted keep a 2 s round trip going through display sleep, lock and lid-shut. What is wrong is the app-side cost: emitPayload dedupes an identical payload for kHeartbeatInterval = 5.0 s (helper.m:52, 100-108), and the idle/paused payload carries no live clock field (helper.m:210-227 - elapsed/timestamp come from MediaRemote and are static while paused), so the app is woken to parse a line roughly every 6 s, not every 2 s. Also worth naming: the helper's NSTimer sets no tolerance, which is the actual Apple-guidance miss (macOS coalesces timers by their tolerance; every other timer in the app sets one). | corrected: The perl helper wakes and does a MediaRemote XPC round trip every 2 s for the life of the app, with no suspend path and nothing in the display-sleep/lock handlers standing it down - but because emitPayload suppresses an unchanged payload for 5 s, a paused or empty session only wakes the Swift side to parse NDJSON about once every 6 s. Measured cost is ~0.1% of a core, so the defect is the un-stood-down wake-ups (and the tolerance-less helper timer), not a per-2-s parse on the main actor. ;; Mechanism confirmed: MediaController.setActive(false) (MediaController.swift:254-286) never touches the feed — feed.stop() exists only in MediaController.stop() (MediaController.swift:241), reached solely from NotchStores.stop() at app quit, and NotchStores.suspendForIdleScreen (NotchStores.swift:82-85) calls only clipboard.stop() + media.setActive(false). So the 2 s NSTimer at helper.m:414 and the perl↔mediaremoted round trip do survive display sleep, contradicting the sleep handler's own comment at NotchController.swift:61-64 ("every poll stops with it"). But the volume is wrong: emitPayload dedupes identical payloads behind kHeartbeatInterval = 5.0 (helper.m:52, 103-106), so an idle session writes one line every ~6 s, not every 2 s — the app does not parse a snapshot every 2 s. Cost by the claim's own measurement is 0.18 s CPU / 180 s = 0.1 % of one core, and with the lid shut the whole system suspends and no timer fires at all. Nobody notices 0.1 % of a core; it is an energy-hygiene gap and an inconsistency with the stated sleep contract, not something a user complains about. | corrected: minor — the MediaRemote helper's 2 s poll (helper.m:414) is the one sampler the display-sleep path does not stand down (NotchStores.swift:82-85), so perl and mediaremoted keep exchanging a round trip while the screen is dark; output is deduped to a 5 s heartbeat so the app parses ~1 line per 6 s, and the measured cost is ~0.1 % of a core.
- [CONFIRMED] (major/S) Every visit to the Shelf tab re-stats up to 60 files on the main actor and regenerates every thumbnail from scratch
    today: Switching to Shelf blocks the main actor on up to 60 reachability checks, then fires up to 60 QuickLook requests that re-render thumbnails the store already had, and publishes 60 separate array mutations as they land. Switching away and back does all of it again.
    apple: Finder and Quick Look cache thumbnails keyed by file identity plus modification date and never regenerate one that has not changed. Reachability and other `NSURL` resource values for files that may live on iCloud, a network share or an external volume are fetched off the main queue — `checkResourceIsReachable` on a dataless iCloud placeholder can materialise the file, and on an unresponsive SMB mount it blocks for the mount's timeout.
    impact: Tabbing to Shelf hitches, and hitches again every time. Worse, one iCloud-evicted or network file in the shelf stalls the whole app: the pointer sampler, the open/close spring and the media position clock all live on the same actor, so the island freezes mid-animation with no beachball to explain it (the app has no window to spin one on). Repeated visits also respawn quicklookd preview extensions for PDFs and video, which shows in Activity Monitor as CPU the user cannot attribute to Isla.
    evidence: `Sources/IslaKit/Model/NotchViewModel.swift:58` — `if tab == .shelf { shelf.refreshFromDisk() }` in the `tab` `didSet`, so it runs on every switch to the tab (ShelfPane.swift has no `onAppear`; this is the only trigger, and it fires every time). `Sources/IslaKit/Services/ShelfStore.swift:115-123`: `let gone = Set(items.filter { Self.isGone($0.url) }.map(\.id))` … `items.forEach(loadThumbnail)`. `isGone` (ShelfStore.swift:135) is `try !url.checkResourceIsReachable()` — a synchronous filesystem round trip on `@MainActor`, for up to `limit = 60` items (ShelfStore.swift:39). `loadThumbnail` (ShelfStore.swift:153-172) builds a fresh `QLThumbnailGenerator.Request` per item with no cache and no guard for an icon already loaded, and each completion writes `self.items[index].icon = image` individually into the `@Published` array — up to 60 separate view-graph invalidations.
    fix: In `ShelfStore`, cache generated thumbnails as `[URL: (mtime: Date, image: NSImage)]` and make `loadThumbnail` return early when the cached entry's modification date still matches, so a revisit costs nothing. Move the `isGone` sweep to `DispatchQueue.global(qos: .utility)` and hop back to the main actor with only the ids to remove — the permission-prompt reasoning in the doc comment at ShelfStore.swift:112 is unaffected, since the prompt is raised by the access, not by the thread. Collect the QuickLook completions and apply them in one `items` assignment instead of 60 individual `@Published` writes.
    challenges: Every code fact holds: NotchViewModel.swift:58 calls shelf.refreshFromDisk() from the tab didSet with no visited-guard (SettingsPane.swift:127 is the only other caller); ShelfStore.swift:116-123 runs the synchronous isGone() check (ShelfStore.swift:135, try url.checkResourceIsReachable() on @MainActor) across up to limit = 60 items (ShelfStore.swift:39) and then unconditionally items.forEach(loadThumbnail); loadThumbnail (ShelfStore.swift:153-172) builds a fresh QLThumbnailGenerator.Request with no in-memory cache and no guard for an icon already present, and each completion writes self.items[index].icon individually into the @Published array. The waste is real. The user-visible cost is not major: reachability on local files is tens of microseconds, so 60 checks are single-digit milliseconds; QuickLook work is async and quicklookd caches across requests; and a typical shelf holds a handful of dropped files, not 60. A stutter on tab switch needs a near-full shelf of heavy media. The sharper risk the claim does not make is the one worth ranking: checkResourceIsReachable on an item living on a dropped network mount blocks the main actor (and therefore the entire UI) for the mount timeout. | corrected: minor — switching to Shelf re-stats every item on the main actor and re-issues every QuickLook thumbnail it already has (NotchViewModel.swift:58, ShelfStore.swift:116-123); pure waste, noticeable only with a near-full 60-item shelf, with a narrower main-actor-hang risk if any item sits on an unreachable network volume.
- [CONFIRMED] (minor/S) Nothing in the app knows that Low Power Mode or thermal pressure exists
    today: A laptop at 8 % battery with Low Power Mode on, or a Mac in `.serious` thermal state with the fans up, gets exactly the same pointer sampling rate, the same 20 fps particle field and the same display-rate karaoke sweep it got at 100 % on mains.
    apple: Low Power Mode is a system-wide instruction to shed discretionary work, and Apple's own software honours it visibly — Safari drops video frame rates and pauses non-essential animation, Mail lengthens fetch intervals, iCloud defers sync. `ProcessInfo.thermalStateDidChangeNotification` exists for the same reason, with explicit guidance to reduce frame rate and background work at `.serious`. An app whose whole premise is that it is always running owes both an answer — the same way it already owes and gives one to Reduce Motion and Reduce Transparency (SystemAppearance.swift, and the `reduceMotion` branches at SpoilerClock.swift:15 and Skeleton.swift:69).
    impact: The user who most needs the app to be cheap — on battery, in Low Power Mode, or with a hot Mac — gets no relief, and the app quietly contradicts a system setting the user deliberately turned on. It is also the cheapest competitive claim to make truthfully in a listing next to NotchNook or Alcove.
    evidence: `rg -n 'isLowPowerMode|thermalState|NSProcessInfoPowerState' Sources Tests` returns no matches anywhere in the repository; the only `ProcessInfo` uses are the `DI_GEOM` / `DI_LOCK_PREVIEW` / `DI_OPEN_LYRICS` verification env vars. Running at full rate regardless of power state: the pointer sampler's fast tier at `1.0 / 60.0` (NotchMetrics.swift:32); `SpoilerClock`'s `TimelineView(.animation(minimumInterval: 1.0 / 20))` (SpoilerClock.swift:19) driving a `Canvas` that lays down `min(Int(size.width * size.height * density), 7000)` ellipses per frame with four `sin`/`cos` per dot (SpoilerField.swift:76, 88-96 — the doc comment records this measured at 35 % CPU before it was tuned down); the 4 Hz position ticker (MediaController.swift:1109); `KaraokeRenderer`, which is `Animatable` and therefore redraws laid-out text at display rate (KaraokeText.swift:18-28); and the 2 s geometry watchdog.
    fix: Add a small `PowerState: ObservableObject` next to `SystemAppearance` publishing `isLowPower` from `ProcessInfo.processInfo.isLowPowerModeEnabled` (observing `.NSProcessInfoPowerStateDidChange`) and `isThermallyPressured` from `.thermalStateDidChangeNotification` (`>= .serious`). Then: `PointerWatcher.fastInterval` becomes `1.0 / 30` when either is true; `SpoilerClock` takes the branch it already has for Reduce Motion (`content.environment(\.spoilerTime, 0)`), so the dust stands still and the 20 Hz timeline disappears entirely; `LyricsStore` network fetches defer; and the geometry watchdog stretches from 2 s to 10 s. Reuse the existing Reduce Motion plumbing rather than inventing a parallel one.
    challenges: The absence is real — rg over Sources/Tests returns no isLowPowerMode / thermalState / NSProcessInfoPowerState match, and the only ProcessInfo reads are the DI_GEOM / DI_LOCK_PREVIEW verification vars (e.g. NotchController.swift:403). But the workloads cited are foreground-only and short-lived, so there is almost nothing for a power-state gate to save. SpoilerField's own header (SpoilerField.swift:57-59) records that the field "is alive only while the panel is open — a folded panel has no content view at all", and the 35 % CPU measurement it quotes was on the notes editor, a surface that was removed; the density has since been cut to 0.4 at 20 fps. KaraokeText's display-rate redraw and the 4 Hz position ticker are likewise gated on the panel being open (MediaController.swift:1106, guard isPlaying, isActive). The pointer sampler already drops to 8 Hz at rest (NotchMetrics.swift:32-34), and the app already honors the accessibility signal that actually exists for this (SpoilerClock.swift:15-17, Theme's Reduce Motion variants). No user would name a missing Low Power Mode branch on an app whose idle cost is a few timers. | corrected: polish — the app reads neither ProcessInfo.isLowPowerMode nor thermalState, but every full-rate path it would gate (spoiler field, karaoke sweep, 4 Hz ticker) only runs while the panel is open, and Reduce Motion is already honored, so the saving is close to nil.
- [CONFIRMED] (minor/S) The geometry watchdog beats every 2 s for the life of the app, including while the screen is asleep and the Mac is locked
    today: Two wake-ups a minute, forever, on a Mac whose screen is off and whose panel cannot be seen, to check whether a window nothing is touching has moved.
    apple: A repair loop that exists to catch a race is armed by the events that cause the race — `didChangeScreenParametersNotification`, `activeSpaceDidChangeNotification`, screen wake, unlock — all four of which this controller already observes (:48, :54, :81, and `lockPresence.onUnlock` at :117). Apple does not ship permanent heartbeats for conditions the system already notifies about.
    impact: Nothing visible. It is pure background cost on a machine nobody is looking at, and it is one of the only two timers still firing after the sleep handler has quietened everything else.
    evidence: `Sources/IslaKit/Notch/NotchController.swift:379-431` — `startGeometryWatchdog()` builds `Timer(timeInterval: 2, repeats: true)` and adds it to `RunLoop.main` in `.common`. `grep -n geometryWatchdog NotchController.swift` shows it assigned at :430 and invalidated only in `teardown` (field declared at :28). It is not gated on `screensAreAsleep`, which this same controller already tracks and sets at :27, :73 and :88 and consults at :285. Each tick reads `panel.frame`, `contentView.bounds` and `contentView.subviews.first?.frame` and compares four rects. The doc comment's own first line — "Armed only under DI_GEOM=1" — is stale: the body runs unconditionally and only the `DebugTrail.note` inside it is env-gated (:403).
    fix: Either add `guard !self.screensAreAsleep else { return }` as the first line of the timer body — one line, keeps the current shape — or invalidate the watchdog in the `screensDidSleepNotification` handler alongside `pointer.stop()` and `stores.suspendForIdleScreen()` (NotchController.swift:71-77) and call `startGeometryWatchdog()` again from the wake handler next to `pointer.start()` (:108). While there, correct the stale first line of the doc comment so the next reader does not believe it is DI_GEOM-only.
    challenges: Facts all check out: startGeometryWatchdog() is called unconditionally from setup at NotchController.swift:123, builds Timer(timeInterval: 2, repeats: true) on RunLoop.main in .common (NotchController.swift:381, 429-430), is invalidated only in teardown, and is never gated on screensAreAsleep even though the same controller tracks that flag (NotchController.swift:27, 73, 88) and consults it at :285. The stale first doc line is there too — "Armed only under DI_GEOM=1." at NotchController.swift:369, above the newer comment, with only the DebugTrail.note at :403 actually env-gated. But the per-tick work is three rect reads and four float comparisons with an early return, and timer.tolerance = 0.5 (NotchController.swift:428) lets the system coalesce the wake-up into ones it was already making. There is no measurable and no perceptible consequence; it is a consistency nit against the app's own "every poll stops with a dark display" contract, plus a misleading comment. | corrected: polish — the geometry watchdog's 2 s timer is not gated on screensAreAsleep like the other pollers are (NotchController.swift:381 vs :73), and its first doc line at :369 falsely says it is armed only under DI_GEOM=1; the per-tick cost is three rect comparisons with 0.5 s tolerance, so nothing is measurably lost.
- [DISPUTED] (minor/M) Opening the panel spikes the process footprint by ~130 MB and leaves it at 1.7× its closed size
    today: One panel open allocates and frees on the order of 110–130 MB, and the process does not return to its 27.7 MB closed footprint afterwards. A 52 pt Gaussian is being asked for at the artwork's native resolution — MediaRemote covers arrive up to 1400×1400.
    apple: `NSVisualEffectView` and `CIGaussianBlur` at large radii work on a downsampled copy, because a 52 pt blur destroys everything above a few pixels of detail anyway; Apple never blurs a backdrop at source resolution. A decorative layer that changes once per track is rasterized once, not re-composited on every invalidation of its parent.
    impact: On an 8 GB Mac already in yellow memory pressure — the machines these apps are bought for — every panel open is a compressor event, which shows as a stutter in the open animation and as swap paid for later. It also makes the app's Memory column read 48 MB rather than 28 MB for the rest of the session.
    evidence: Measured on this machine against `build/Isla.app`. Normally launched, panel never opened: `vmmap -summary` reports `Physical footprint: 27.7M` / `Physical footprint (peak): 28.0M`. Launched with `DI_OPEN_LYRICS=1` (panel pinned open, lyrics stage on screen), `sample`'s process header reports `Physical footprint: 48.3M` / `Physical footprint (peak): 160.7M`. The likely owner is unbounded: `Sources/IslaKit/UI/GlassSurface.swift:68-129` composes `VibrancyBackdrop(material: .hudWindow)`, then the full artwork `Image` at `.aspectRatio(contentMode: .fill)` under `.blur(radius: 52).saturation(0.72).opacity(0.28)`, then four `LinearGradient`s and a 96 px grain tile — with no `.drawingGroup()` on the light layer and no downscale of the artwork before the 52 pt blur, inside a 700×444 window.
    fix: Downsample the artwork to roughly the blur's effective resolution before handing it to `.blur` — a 64×64 `NSImage` blurred by 52 pt is indistinguishable at 700 pt — and cache that small copy on `MediaController` alongside `artwork` (it is already decoded off-main at MediaController.swift:777-799, so build the thumbnail in the same background hop). Wrap the light layer in `.drawingGroup()` so it rasterizes once per track instead of per parent invalidation. Confirm attribution with Instruments' Allocations before and after: the 130 MB number is measured, but its ownership is inferred from the code rather than proven.
    challenges: The stated mechanism is refuted: `grep -rn 'light:' Sources Tests` shows the `light:` parameter is only ever declared/forwarded inside GlassSurface.swift (61,198,208,221,286) and passed nil in tests — no call site anywhere passes artwork, so the `if let light` branch with `.blur(radius: 52).saturation(0.72).opacity(0.28)` (GlassSurface.swift:73-82) never executes. There are only three glassSurface call sites (LockScreenCard.swift:105 and :381, LyricsStage.swift:336), none with a light image, and on this machine (macOS 26.5.2) GlassSurfaceStyle resolves to Apple's `glassEffect` for backdrop-sampling surfaces (GlassSurface.swift:230-250), so the drawn recipe only paints the lyrics pill (samplesBackdrop: false). The only full-resolution artwork blur that does run is LyricsStage.swift:164-170 (`.blur(radius: 60).opacity(0.35)` over an uncapped-dimension NSImage; ingestion caps bytes only, NowPlayingPayloadDecoder.swift:51 maxArtworkBytes = 4 MB). NotchMetrics.swift:25 maximumWindow = 700×444 checks out. My own measurement of build/Isla.app: closed 24.5 MB footprint / 28.2 MB peak; relaunched with DI_OPEN_LYRICS=1, 40.7 MB / peak 41.0 MB — the ~1.7× resting growth reproduces, the ~130 MB spike did not (my run had no live session/artwork, so the LyricsStage blur was skipped). | corrected: Opening the panel leaves the process at roughly 1.7× its closed footprint (measured 24.5 MB → 40.7 MB here), but the ~130 MB peak did not reproduce without artwork, and it cannot come from GlassSurface's 52 pt light blur — `light:` is never non-nil, and on macOS 26 the panel's surfaces use Apple's glassEffect. The candidate owner is the un-downscaled artwork under `.blur(radius: 60)` at LyricsStage.swift:168. ;; The named owner cannot be executing. GlassSurface's artwork branch (GlassSurface.swift:71-80, the .blur(radius: 52).saturation(0.72).opacity(0.28) stack) only runs when `light` is non-nil, and no production call site ever passes it: `rg -n 'light:' Sources Tests` outside GlassSurface.swift matches only two tests passing `light: nil`; the four real .glassSurface(...) call sites (LyricsStage.swift:336, LockScreenCard.swift:105 and :381) all omit it, and LockScreenCard.swift:105 explicitly documents taking no cover tint. It is dead code. On this machine it is doubly dead: sw_vers reports macOS 26.5.2, so GlassSurfaceStyle.usesSystemGlass is true (GlassSurface.swift:230-237) and the drawn recipe is bypassed for glassEffect entirely. The mechanism sentence is also wrong on its own terms - SwiftUI's .blur applies a Gaussian to the rasterized layer at its layout size (bounded by the 700x444 window), not 'at the artwork's native resolution'; what is actually native-sized is the decoded NSImage (MediaController.swift:791-793, from a payload capped at 4 MB by NowPlayingPayloadDecoder.swift:51). And the measurement does not measure what the headline says: DI_OPEN_LYRICS=1 pins the panel open AND opens the lyrics stage, whose own ambience does Image(nsImage: artwork).resizable().aspectRatio(.fill).blur(radius: 60) (LyricsStage.swift:164-170) - the real candidate - while 'does not return to its closed footprint afterwards' was never measured (no open-then-close reading exists). I reran it against build/Isla.app: idle, panel never opened, vmmap -summary gave Physical footprint 24.4M / peak 28.2M (matching their baseline); relaunched with DI_OPEN_LYRICS=1 it gave 38.1M / peak 38.2M - no ~130 MB spike, though this Mac had no MediaRemote session so no artwork was loaded. | corrected: There is no evidence that opening the panel costs ~130 MB, and the cited cause is unreachable code. What is true: artwork is decoded and retained at full native pixel size with no downscale (MediaController.swift:791-793, source data capped at 4 MB), and the one artwork blur that actually runs is LyricsStage.swift:168 (radius 60, no .drawingGroup()), on the lyrics stage only. Any footprint work should start by profiling that path with a real now-playing session, open-then-close. ;; The named owner is dead code. GlassSurface's `light` parameter defaults to nil (GlassSurface.swift:198) and not one of the three call sites in the whole app passes it — LockScreenCard.swift:105 and :381, LyricsStage.swift:336 are the only `.glassSurface(` uses — so the `.blur(radius: 52)` artwork branch at GlassSurface.swift:76-80 never executes. The real undownscaled blur is LyricsStage.swift:163-169 (`Image(nsImage: artwork).resizable().aspectRatio(contentMode: .fill).blur(radius: 60)`, no drawingGroup, artwork accepted up to 4 MB per NowPlayingPayloadDecoder.swift:51) — which is precisely the surface DI_OPEN_LYRICS=1 pins open, so the measurement characterizes the default-off lyrics feature, not "opening the panel". The "does not return to 27.7 MB afterwards" conclusion is not supported by the evidence given: two different processes were compared with two different tools (vmmap -summary on a never-opened launch vs the sample header on a lyrics launch), and no open-then-close-then-remeasure was done. On the numbers themselves, a 48 MB resident accessory app with a transient 160 MB peak is better than the paid competitors, and no user names it. | corrected: polish — the lyrics stage blurs the cover at its native resolution with a 60 pt radius and no downscale or drawingGroup (LyricsStage.swift:163-169), which is the plausible owner of the ~160 MB peak measured under DI_OPEN_LYRICS=1; GlassSurface.swift:76-80 is not involved because no caller ever passes `light`, and the claimed failure to return to the closed footprint was never measured on a single process.
- [DISPUTED] (polish/M) The pointer sampler never goes fully quiet — an 8 Hz floor runs forever, even on a Mac nobody has touched for hours
    today: Eight `+[NSEvent mouseLocation]` reads per second, forever, on a machine whose cursor has not moved since last night.
    apple: AppKit's own hover handling is event-driven — `NSTrackingArea`, which this very file already uses for the cursor rect and the locked-hover nudge (NotchRootView.swift:35-53, 108-119). Nothing in the system samples the cursor on a heartbeat.
    impact: In practice almost nothing, and I would rather say so than inflate it. A 30 s `sample` of the idle process caught the main thread blocked in `mach_msg` for effectively all of ~8,600 samples, with a single `+[NSEvent mouseLocation]` frame among them; the whole process measures 0.33 % of one core and 10 idle wake-ups in 60 s. This is a structural nit, not a battery problem, and should be fixed only after the four items above.
    evidence: `Sources/IslaKit/Notch/NotchMetrics.swift:32-34` — `fastPointerInterval = 1.0 / 60.0`, `idlePointerInterval = 1.0 / 8.0`, `restThreshold = 3.0`. `PointerWatcher.updateRate` (PointerWatcher.swift:129-144) drops to the idle tier after 3 s without motion but never below it, because `tick()` is also the only thing that sets `panel.ignoresMouseEvents` (PointerWatcher.swift:183 → NotchController.swift:759), and a `nil` from `hitTest` discards a click rather than forwarding it (reasoning stated at NotchController.swift:757). So the poll cannot simply stop. On paper it is 8 of the ~11 timer fires per second the app makes at idle.
    fix: After N seconds without motion (reuse `lastMovedAt`), invalidate the timer entirely and arm `NSEvent.addGlobalMonitorForEvents(matching: [.mouseMoved, .leftMouseDragged, .scrollWheel])` as the wake source, restarting the sampler on the first event and tearing the monitor down again. Mouse-event global monitors need no Accessibility permission — only keyboard ones do — and the blind spot the file header describes (events delivered to this app's own windows) cannot bite: while the panel is collapsed and outside `interactiveRect` it is click-through, and the moment the pointer is over the island the panel is receiving real events and its own `NSTrackingArea` reports entry. Keep the current poll as the in-region path so nothing about hover timing changes.
    challenges: The mechanism is right: NotchMetrics.swift:32-34 defines fast 1/60, idle 1/8, rest 3.0; PointerWatcher.updateRate (PointerWatcher.swift:127-144) only toggles between the two tiers and never below idle; tick() (PointerWatcher.swift:179-186) is the sole source of onInteractiveChange, wired to `panel?.ignoresMouseEvents = !interactive` at NotchController.swift:758-759 with the hitTest reasoning at :756-757; and the idle-timer arithmetic checks out (8/s pointer + 2/s clipboard at ClipboardStore.swift:101 + 0.5/s geometry + 0.25/s feed watchdog ≈ 11/s). But 'runs forever … a cursor that has not moved since last night' is wrong: PointerWatcher.stop() invalidates the timer (PointerWatcher.swift:82-96) and is called on screensDidSleep (NotchController.swift:77) and on both lock branches (:179, :197), and restart is withheld while the screen is still dark (:285-286). The idle timer also carries tolerance = interval/2 = 62.5 ms (PointerWatcher.swift:110), so the system folds those wake-ups into others. | corrected: The sampler has an 8 Hz floor that cannot drop further while the display is awake — because tick() is the only thing that flips panel.ignoresMouseEvents — but it does stop entirely on display sleep and on lock, and its idle timer allows 62.5 ms of tolerance so the wake-ups coalesce. ;; The floor itself is exactly as described: NotchMetrics.swift:32-34 (fast 1/60, idle 1/8, rest 3.0), PointerWatcher.updateRate (:127-144) only ever switches between the two tiers and never stops, and tick() (:180-200) is also the only thing that toggles panel.ignoresMouseEvents via onInteractiveChange (NotchController.swift:758-760) - the reasoning at NotchController.swift:756-757 is quoted correctly - so the poll genuinely cannot just stop without redesigning click-through. The idle arithmetic also holds: 8 Hz pointer + 2 Hz clipboard (ClipboardStore.swift:101, 0.5 s) + 0.5 Hz geometry watchdog + 0.25 Hz silence watchdog is the '~11 fires per second'.
